Upgrading Keycloak

This guide describes how to upgrade Keycloak. Use the following procedures in this order:

  1. Review the migration changes from the previous version of Keycloak.

  2. Upgrade the Keycloak server.

  3. Upgrade the Keycloak adapters.

  4. Upgrade the Keycloak Client Libraries (Admin client, Authorization client, Policy enforcer). These are released independently of the Keycloak server and could be typically updated independently of the Keycloak server as the last released version of the client libraries should be compatible with the last released version of the Keycloak server. For more information, see the Upgrading Keycloak Client libraries.

Migration Changes

Migrating to 27.0.0

Breaking changes

Breaking changes are identified as those that might require changes for existing users to their configurations or applications. In minor or patch releases, Keycloak will only introduce breaking changes to fix bugs.

PAR metadata omitted when PAR is disabled

When the par feature is disabled, OpenID Connect discovery now omits pushed_authorization_request_endpoint from both the top-level metadata and mtls_endpoint_aliases, and omits require_pushed_authorization_requests. Clients must tolerate these absent properties. Enable the par feature if clients require PAR.

Validation of client URI fields

Client URI validation and client policies now share a catalog of URI fields. Existing policy configuration keys, selected field names, regular expressions, and wildcard options retain their meaning. The following additional checks can reject previously accepted client configurations:

  • With Client URIs Must Match enabled, the Trusted Hosts client registration policy checks all supplied URI fields on creation and update, including post-logout redirects, logout endpoints, web origins, JWKS and request URIs, CIBA notification endpoints, logo, policy, terms of service, sector identifier attributes, and SAML endpoints and metadata URLs. Add any legitimate external hosts to the trusted-host list before updating these clients. Post-logout * is rejected because it permits redirects to any host. Explicit URLs accompanying + or - are also checked.

  • The Secure Client URIs executor additionally requires HTTPS without wildcards for sector identifier attributes and SAML endpoints and metadata URLs, regardless of the configured client protocol.

  • The SAML Secure Client URIs executor checks all catalog fields on SAML clients, including stored metadata during login and logout. Replace HTTP URLs with HTTPS URLs or remove unused values before upgrading with this executor enabled or enabling it for the first time. Stored * or null web origins and embedded data: logos also fail its HTTPS check, even if they are unused by SAML. The allow-wildcard-redirects option still applies only to redirect URIs.

  • General client validation additionally checks web origins, sector identifier attributes, and supplied SAML URL attributes even on clients using another protocol. Malformed or disallowed values must be corrected before saving or importing those clients.

These changes close gaps where a URL could evade a policy simply because it was stored in a different client field. A policy configuration migration is not required, but continuing to accept those previously unchecked URLs would leave the gaps open. Relative URLs, embedded data: logos, and web origin markers +, *, and null retain their meaning in general validation and Trusted Hosts. The HTTPS executors still enforce their own scheme restrictions. General validation also preserves the legacy web origin literals ${authAdminUrl}, ${authBaseUrl}, and file://. These values remain subject to the host, HTTPS, and pattern restrictions of any enabled client policies. Trusted Hosts resolves relative logo URLs against the Keycloak frontend URL and checks that host against its allowlist. The client’s root URL does not determine where the browser loads a logo. The pattern executor continues to match literal configured values, including markers and relative URLs, and respects an explicit field selection. Sector identifier URLs in pairwise protocol mappers remain subject to their separate validation.

Authorization endpoint JSON errors

When an OpenID Connect authorization request with Accept: application/json is rejected during client or request validation, the JSON response now contains an OAuth error code in error and the human-readable message in error_description. For example, an unknown client returns {"error":"invalid_client","error_description":"Client not found."}. Applications should use error rather than matching translated messages. HTTP status codes and HTML error pages retain their existing behavior, and errors sent to a validated redirect URI continue to use that redirect. Other JSON error pages, including direct-grant authenticator errors, are unchanged.

Identity brokering API V1 token retrieval requires the client to be allowed for the identity provider

The V1 identity brokering token endpoint (GET /realms/{realm}/broker/{provider_alias}/token) previously only required the user to have the broker.read-token role, which allowed a user linked to one identity provider to retrieve stored tokens from any other identity provider in the realm.

The endpoint now additionally requires the client for which the bearer token was issued to be allowed to retrieve external tokens from the requested identity provider, which is the same authorization model as used by the V2 endpoint. Existing V1 clients without this configuration receive an HTTP 403 response when retrieving a stored token. To continue using the V1 endpoint, enable Allow retrieve external tokens in the client’s Capability config and add the identity provider to Allowed Identity Providers for External Tokens.

While V1 is enabled, these settings are also shown in the Admin Console for public clients. V2 hides them for public clients because it only supports confidential clients.

Realm-level default client scopes require manage-realm

Changing the realm-level default and optional client scopes through the Admin REST API endpoints default-default-client-scopes/{clientScopeId} and default-optional-client-scopes/{clientScopeId} now requires the manage-realm role. Previously manage-clients was sufficient, even though these lists determine which client scopes every newly created client in the realm receives. Administrators who hold only manage-clients can still create, update, and delete individual client scopes, and can still read the realm-level lists; they can no longer change which of them are realm defaults. If you have automation that manages realm default client scopes with a manage-clients account, grant it manage-realm or run it with an account that already has it.

Client scope management restored for per-client delegated administrators

The additional client scope permission checks introduced for CVE-2026-14614 have been removed from client scope assignment endpoints. Those checks incorrectly restricted the intended delegated client administration model and prevented administrators from managing scope assignments for their clients.

With Fine-Grained Admin Permissions (FGAP), a per-client view permission allows an administrator to see the scopes assigned to that client, including their descriptions and parameterized-scope indicators. A per-client manage permission allows assigning any existing realm client scope to that client, removing assignments, and changing the assigned type (default ↔ optional). Global manage-clients or type-level Clients manage permission is no longer required for these operations. In FGAP v2, manage does not imply view; grant both permissions to manage scope assignments through the Admin Console.

The Admin Console obtains summaries of available scopes from GET …​/clients/{id}/available-client-scopes, which requires manage permission on that client. This does not grant access to protocol mapper configurations, other scope attributes, or permission to edit shared client scope definitions. The realm-wide client scope catalog retains its existing permissions.

Grant per-client manage only to administrators trusted to assign any existing realm client scope to that client, including the scope’s protocol mappers and role scope mappings. This change does not introduce restrictions on which scopes a delegated administrator may assign.

Deleting required client scopes

Deleting a required client scope now retains the requirement. The policy and any enclosing aggregated policies deny access while the scope is missing, regardless of positive or negative logic. A warning identifies affected policies; an authorization administrator can remove or replace the missing reference to resume normal evaluation. Requirements removed before upgrading cannot be restored automatically.

The Java authorization SPI adds Decision.Effect.INVALID for invalid policy configuration. The partial-evaluation SPI adds the equivalent Outcome.INVALID. Custom extensions must handle these new enum values: aggregated policies propagate them unchanged, while permission decision strategies count them as a denial. Admin REST API evaluation responses continue to report only PERMIT or DENY.

Realm client session statistics respect client visibility

The Admin REST API endpoint GET /admin/realms/{realm}/client-session-stats now returns online and offline session statistics only for clients the caller is allowed to view. It continues to require the view-realm or manage-realm role. Previously, either realm role allowed callers to retrieve statistics for clients they could not view. After upgrading, such callers receive fewer entries, or an empty list if no clients with sessions are visible to them.

Client visibility follows the same permission checks as the client-specific session count endpoints across RBAC, fine-grained admin permissions v1, and v2:

  • The view-clients and manage-clients admin roles grant visibility to all clients.

  • Fine-grained admin permissions v1 grant client visibility through the view, configure, or manage permission.

  • Fine-grained admin permissions v2 keep the Clients view and manage scopes independent. When access is granted only through fine-grained permissions, manage alone does not grant visibility.

Counts for visible clients continue to include all their sessions, independently of the caller’s permission to view the associated users.

SAML logins require a valid Bearer SubjectConfirmationData

When processing a SAML assertion received from an identity provider, Keycloak now rejects the login when none of the assertion’s Bearer SubjectConfirmation elements carries a valid SubjectConfirmationData element, as required by the SAML 2.0 Web Browser SSO Profile. This affects both SAML identity provider brokering and applications secured with the Keycloak SAML adapter.

The following conditions are now enforced:

  • The assertion’s Subject must contain a SubjectConfirmation with Method set to Bearer, and it must carry a SubjectConfirmationData element. A missing SubjectConfirmation, or one without SubjectConfirmationData, is now rejected; previously it was silently ignored. If the assertion carries multiple Bearer confirmations, they are treated as alternatives and only one of them needs to validate.

  • SubjectConfirmationData must carry a NotOnOrAfter attribute.

  • SubjectConfirmationData must carry a Recipient attribute matching the response’s Destination, whenever the response specifies one.

  • For broker logins only, SubjectConfirmationData must carry an InResponseTo attribute matching the expected request ID, for SP-initiated logins.

After upgrading, logins through a SAML identity provider, or through an application using the SAML adapter, fail when the assertion omits SubjectConfirmationData on every Bearer confirmation, or omits one of the attributes above when the corresponding check applies. Broker logins fail with an invalid response error; adapter logins restart the login flow. If your identity provider’s responses do not meet these requirements, update it to include a spec-compliant Bearer SubjectConfirmationData.

SAML broker IdP-initiated logins enforce a recipient bound to the target client

When processing a SAML assertion received from an identity provider through identity brokering, Keycloak now always validates the Bearer SubjectConfirmationData Recipient against the broker endpoint expected for that specific login — the per-client IdP-initiated endpoint for IdP-initiated logins, or the generic broker endpoint for SP-initiated logins — regardless of whether the outer SAML response Destination is present.

Previously, when the identity provider’s response omitted the (optional, and not necessarily signed) Destination attribute, the Recipient attribute was not validated at all for broker logins. This allowed an assertion that an identity provider legitimately issued for one IdP-initiated client to be replayed, unmodified, against a different IdP-initiated client configured on the same broker, bypassing client-specific login policy.

After upgrading, a Bearer SubjectConfirmationData whose Recipient is missing or does not match the expected broker endpoint is rejected, even when the response’s Destination is absent or matches. If you operate an identity provider that issues assertions without a Recipient, or with a Recipient that does not point at the expected Keycloak broker endpoint, update it to send a spec-compliant Bearer SubjectConfirmationData.

SAML logins with an empty principal are rejected

When processing a SAML assertion received from an identity provider, Keycloak now rejects the login when the principal that identifies the user is empty or contains only whitespace. Depending on the Principal type of the identity provider, the principal is the subject NameID or the value of the configured attribute. Accounts whose link to the identity provider has an empty user ID can no longer log in through it; remove this link so that their owners can link the accounts again once the identity provider sends a non-empty principal.

SAML back-channel SLO via SessionIndex requires client signature

Back-channel SAML single logout that identifies the session with SessionIndex (no browser cookie) now requires the initiating client to have Client signature required (saml.client.signature) enabled. Without a verified signature, the Issuer field is not authenticated and could be forged to terminate another client’s session.

Browser-based (front-channel) logout that carries the user’s SSO cookie is unchanged and still works for clients that do not require signatures.

If a SAML client uses back-channel SLO with SessionIndex and has Client signature required set to OFF, either:

  • Enable Client signature required and configure the client’s signing certificate, then sign LogoutRequest messages, or

  • Prefer front-channel logout so the browser cookie authenticates the request.

Newly created SAML clients already default to requiring client signatures. Deployments that import realm JSON may still have unsigned clients and are the primary group affected by this change.

Email verification before domain-based organization membership

Automatic membership through a linked identity provider now requires a verified email address when the organization has configured domains. If the address is unverified, the first broker login flow requires email verification before granting membership, even when the realm’s Verify email setting is disabled.

Configure the realm’s email server so users can complete verification, or enable Trust Email for an identity provider whose email verification you trust. An OpenID Connect provider returning email_verified=false, or a user changing their address during profile review, still requires local verification.

Existing memberships, organizations without domains, and invitation-based membership are unaffected. If verification is completed in another browser or the original authentication session is lost, users must sign in through the identity provider again and follow the account-linking prompts. Local verification is preserved for a limited time on this retry with the same identity provider account and unchanged email address, including with FORCE synchronization. Identity provider mappers using IMPORT are not replayed during this retry.

Custom identity providers that synchronize email outside AbstractIdentityProvider should implement UserAuthenticationIdentityProvider.updateBrokeredEmail to expose their email verification policy.

Admin client organizations.findOne no longer rejects when the organization does not exist

The organizations.findOne method in the @keycloak/keycloak-admin-client package now resolves to undefined when the organization does not exist, instead of rejecting with a 404 error. Its declared return type changes from OrganizationRepresentation to OrganizationRepresentation | undefined.

This aligns the method with the equivalent lookups on other resources, such as groups.findOne, roles.findOneById, and clients.findOne, which already resolve to undefined for a missing entity.

After upgrading, code that relies on a rejected promise to detect a missing organization no longer takes that path. TypeScript users see a compilation error wherever the result is used without a check. Plain JavaScript users receive undefined instead of an error, and may only observe the change at a later point in their code.

Replace any handling that catches a 404 from this method with a check for undefined:

const organization = await adminClient.organizations.findOne({ id });

if (!organization) {
  // handle the missing organization
}
Mandatory signature validation for OIDC identity provider backchannel logout tokens

Keycloak now always validates the signature of backchannel logout tokens received from external OIDC identity providers, regardless of the Validate Signatures setting on the identity provider configuration. This change addresses CVE-2026-18569, where an unauthenticated attacker could forge unsigned logout tokens (using alg=none) to force-logout targeted users when signature validation was disabled.

The OIDC Back-Channel Logout 1.0 specification mandates that logout tokens MUST be signed and that alg=none is explicitly prohibited. Keycloak now enforces this requirement.

Other token types (ID tokens, access tokens) continue to respect the Validate Signatures setting as before.

If your OIDC identity provider has Validate Signatures set to OFF and uses backchannel logout, you must configure the identity provider’s public key so that Keycloak can validate logout token signatures. Either:

  • Set Use JWKS URL to ON and configure the JWKS URL pointing to the external IDP’s JWKS endpoint, or

  • Provide the IDP’s public key directly via the Validating Public Key field and, if needed, its key ID via the Validating Public Key Id field.

Identity provider client secret must be re-entered when changing its destination (CVE-2026-15943)

When reading an OpenID Connect, OAuth 2.0, or social identity provider through the Admin REST API, the clientSecret is returned masked as **********. Previously, an update that sent this masked value back always kept the stored secret, even if the request also changed where that secret is sent. A user with the manage-identity-providers role could therefore point the identity provider at an attacker-controlled token endpoint and have Keycloak send the real client secret there.

Starting with this release, the stored secret is kept only when the fields that determine where and how it is sent are unchanged: clientId, clientAuthMethod, tokenUrl, tokenIntrospectionUrl, and baseUrl (GitHub and OpenShift), as well as tenantId (Microsoft) and sandbox (PayPal). If any of these change while the request still contains the masked value, the update is rejected with HTTP 400 Bad Request and the identity provider is left unchanged. To apply such a change, include the actual clientSecret in the request. The Admin Console clears the Client Secret field automatically when one of these fields is edited so that you can enter it again.

The providerId of an existing identity provider can no longer be changed through an update request; sending a different value is rejected with HTTP 400 Bad Request.

Scripts and tools that read an identity provider, modify one of the fields above, and write it back (for example, kcadm.sh update identity-provider/instances/<alias> -s config.tokenUrl=…​) must now also pass the client secret (-s config.clientSecret=…​). Updates that change other settings, or that use a ${vault.<key>} expression as the secret, are not affected.

Custom identity providers whose secret is sent to a destination derived from provider-specific configuration can take part in this check by overriding IdentityProviderModel#canReuseMaskedClientSecret in the model returned by IdentityProviderFactory#createConfig(). Providers extending OAuth2IdentityProviderConfig can instead override getClientSecretDestinationConfigKeys() and isClientSecretDestinationChanged().

Login v1 theme removed

The legacy Login v1 theme (keycloak) and the login:v1 feature have been removed. PatternFly 3 and PatternFly 4 vendor resources that only served that theme are also removed.

Realms and clients that still had the login theme set to keycloak are migrated to keycloak.v2 on upgrade. Custom themes that extended keycloak for the login type must be updated to extend keycloak.v2 or base instead. The --features=login:v1 and --features-disabled=login:v1 options are no longer valid. The login feature itself can no longer be disabled; Keycloak always provides the keycloak.v2 login theme.

CIBA requires client authentication (CVE-2026-109082)

The Client Initiated Backchannel Authentication (CIBA) grant is now rejected for public clients, as required by the CIBA specification, which mandates client authentication on both the backchannel authentication endpoint and the token endpoint. Previously, a public client with the CIBA grant enabled could complete the flow without authenticating and obtain tokens for the approving user.

SCIM listings respect per-resource view permissions (CVE-2026-116470)

Listing and searching resources through the SCIM API now returns only the resources the caller is allowed to view. Previously, the query-users and query-groups roles were checked when entering GET /realms/{realm}/scim/v2/Users, GET /realms/{realm}/scim/v2/Groups, and their .search counterparts, but the resources those endpoints matched were returned in full without a per-resource view check. A service account holding only query-users could therefore read every user in the realm, including email addresses, names, and custom attributes, which the Admin REST API would not have disclosed to it.

The query roles now behave as they do in the Admin REST API: they grant access to the listing and search endpoints, not read access to the resources those endpoints match. After upgrading, a client holding only query-users or query-groups receives an empty Resources array. Grant view-users in addition, or configure a fine-grained view permission on the users and groups the client is allowed to see. When fine-grained admin permissions v2 are enabled for the realm, listings were already narrowed to the permitted resources and are unaffected.

With fine-grained admin permissions v2, the listing and the count are restricted by the same authorization filter in the database query, so totalResults stays exact. With the deprecated fine-grained admin permissions v1, a caller can be allowed to view some resources but not others, so totalResults counts the resources matching the query, before the per-resource view check is applied, and should be treated as an upper bound; a page can also be shorter than the requested count, or even empty, while later pages still contain resources the caller is allowed to see, so neither a short page nor an empty page means that no further resources exist, and clients should keep incrementing startIndex by count until startIndex exceeds totalResults. Without any fine-grained admin permissions, the per-resource view check evaluates the same way for every resource of a given type, so access is all-or-nothing: a client holding only a query role and no realm-wide view role gets a totalResults of 0, consistently across every page, rather than the unfiltered database count.

Service account user endpoint removed from Admin REST API

The GET /admin/realms/{realm}/clients/{id}/service-account-user endpoint has been removed. The service account user ID is now available directly on the client representation as the serviceAccountUserId field, which is populated when serviceAccountsEnabled is true. This field is read-only and managed by the server.

If you previously called this endpoint to obtain the service account user ID for role mapping or other operations, retrieve the client representation instead:

// Before:
UserRepresentation sa = clientResource.getServiceAccountUser();
String saUserId = sa.getId();

// After:
String saUserId = clientResource.toRepresentation().getServiceAccountUserId();

The getServiceAccountUser() method has also been removed from the Java and JavaScript admin client libraries. If you need the full user representation for the service account, use the user ID from the client representation to fetch it via the Users API.

Notable changes

Notable changes may include internal behavior changes that prevent common misconfigurations, bugs that are fixed, or changes to simplify running Keycloak. It also lists significant changes to internal APIs.

SAML assertions with a OneTimeUse condition are no longer replayable

When Keycloak acts as a SAML service provider through identity brokering, an assertion that contains a OneTimeUse condition is now accepted only once. Previously, the condition was parsed but not enforced, so the same assertion could be presented again within its validity period.

After upgrading, a replayed assertion is rejected with an error page and an IDENTITY_PROVIDER_RESPONSE_ERROR event, and the user is not logged in. The event has a plain-text reason detail that explains why the assertion was rejected, for example that it was already used or that it exceeds the maximum age. This affects only identity providers that include the condition in their assertions, including a Keycloak realm acting as the identity provider when the Include OneTimeUse Condition option is enabled on the SAML client. Assertions without the condition are not affected. The new OneTimeUse maximum age setting of the SAML identity provider defaults to 1 hour (3,600 seconds). Assertions with a OneTimeUse condition that are older than this are rejected. Increase the setting if users need more time to complete a login with such an assertion.

Scope mapping listings filter roles by visibility

The Admin REST API endpoints for reading the scope mappings of a client or a client scope (GET /admin/realms/{realm}/clients/{id}/scope-mappings and GET /admin/realms/{realm}/client-scopes/{id}/scope-mappings, including their realm, realm/composite, clients/{client} and clients/{client}/composite sub-resources) now filter out roles that the caller is allowed neither to view nor to map to users or to client scopes. Previously, any caller with permission to view the client or the client scope could see all the roles in its scope, including realm roles without the view-realm role and client roles without a view permission on the client they belong to. After upgrading, callers may see fewer results from these endpoints if the scope contains roles that they are allowed neither to view nor to map. Roles that the caller is allowed only to map to client scopes are returned without their attributes, even when briefRepresentation=false is requested. For example, an administrator with the view-clients role but without the view-realm role no longer sees the realm roles in the scope of a client, unless they are allowed to map these roles to users, for example through the manage-users role, or to client scopes. With fine-grained admin permissions V2, an administrator with a view permission on a client no longer sees the roles of other clients in its scope, unless they are also allowed to view these other clients, or to map their roles to users (map-role on the role or map-roles on the client) or to client scopes (map-role-client-scope on the role or map-roles-client-scope on the client). In the Admin Console, these roles are no longer listed in the Scope tab of client scopes and of dedicated client scopes.

The client scope evaluation endpoints (GET /admin/realms/{realm}/clients/{id}/evaluate-scopes/scope-mappings/{role-container}/granted and not-granted) apply the same role visibility rules under both legacy RBAC and fine-grained admin permissions V2. After upgrading, callers may see fewer evaluated role scope mappings when they cannot view or map roles to users or client scopes in the requested realm or client role container. Callers allowed only to map roles to client scopes can still see those roles, and roles in the effective scope appear under granted rather than not-granted.

Group lookup by path filters role mappings by visibility

The Admin REST API endpoint GET /admin/realms/{realm}/group-by-path/{path} now omits the role mappings that the caller is not allowed to view or to map to users, as the groups/{id} endpoint and the group listings already do. Previously, any caller allowed to view the group received all of its role mappings in realmRoles and clientRoles. For example, an administrator with only the view-users role no longer receives them, while an administrator with the manage-users role still does. If an integration reads group role mappings through this endpoint, make sure its account is allowed to view the roles, for example through the view-realm and view-clients roles or the corresponding fine-grained permissions.

Workflow expressions with unsupported characters are rejected

Workflow expressions used in the on, if and concurrency settings are now rejected when they contain characters that are not part of the expression syntax, such as !. Previously, these characters were silently dropped, so an expression like !has-role(admin) was accepted and evaluated as has-role(admin). Use the not, and and or operators instead, for example not has-role(admin). After upgrading, existing workflows with such an expression in on or if are no longer executed, and the error is logged when the expression is evaluated. If such an expression is used in a concurrency setting, the workflow still runs, but that setting no longer restarts or cancels in-progress executions, and the error is logged. Update these workflows to fix their expressions.

Workflow schedule settings are validated

Creating or updating a workflow now fails when schedule.after is not a duration of at least 1 second, or when schedule.batch-size is not greater than 0. Previously, such values caused an internal server error, or they were stored and the scheduled runs failed or did not process any resources. Existing workflows with such values must be fixed before they can be updated, including when enabling or disabling them in the Admin Console.

Organization Redirect URL now validated against the account client

The Redirect URL configured on an organization is now validated against the valid redirect URIs of the account client. If the URL is not listed in the client’s allowed redirect URIs, the user is redirected to the account console instead. When a custom client is specified in the invitation, the organization’s Redirect URL is ignored and the user is redirected to the custom client’s base URL.

Previously, the Redirect URL was used without validation, which could allow an open redirect. After upgrading, if your organization has a custom Redirect URL pointing to your application, you must add that URL to the account client’s Valid Redirect URIs. Otherwise, the redirect will silently fall back to the account console. This does not apply to invitations sent with a custom client, which always use the client’s own base URL.

The invite-existing-user endpoint now also accepts an optional client_id query parameter, the same way invite-user already does. If omitted, both endpoints default to the account client.

The admin-permissions client is treated as an internal client

When fine-grained admin permissions V2 are enabled on a realm, the admin-permissions client holds the whole authorization model of that realm. This client is now treated as an internal client, the same way the realm-management client already is, so it can no longer be managed through the Admin REST API. Previously, an administrator with a manage permission on clients could update it, including changing its clientId, even though the access.manage field returned for the client was already false.

After upgrading, requests that update the admin-permissions client are rejected with a 403 status. Requests that delete it are also rejected with a 403 status, where previously they were rejected with a 400 status. Configuring the permissions and the policies of the realm is not affected, as it requires the manage-realm role rather than a permission on the client.

User storage periodic sync runs at the configured period again

Periodic full and changed-users synchronization for a user storage provider now starts every fullSyncPeriod or changedSyncPeriod seconds, as documented. A regression caused a sync to run only on every other scheduled tick, so the effective interval was about twice the configured period, whenever a single sync took longer than roughly one second. The provider’s last-sync time is now recorded when a sync starts rather than when it finishes, so the schedule no longer drifts by the sync duration.

If you previously lowered fullSyncPeriod or changedSyncPeriod to compensate for the doubled interval, review those values.

Stricter enforcement of the requested level of authentication

Previously, Keycloak could complete a login at a lower level of authentication than the client requested, when the authentication flow skipped the steps for the requested level, usually because the user already had a session. Keycloak now rejects such logins. For OpenID Connect clients, the requested level is the level of an essential acr claim. For SAML clients, it is the level of the requested authentication context or of the minimum ACR value of the client. The level is now enforced in every authentication flow, including flows without a Conditional - Level Of Authentication condition and the registration and reset credentials flows. An active login in a flow without the condition now always reaches at least level 1, and the acr claim reports it accordingly.

A rejected interactive login ends on an error page. A rejected passive login returns the error unmet_authentication_requirements or login_required to an OpenID Connect client and the NoAuthnContext status to a SAML client. For the level that each flow reaches, see Creating a browser login flow with step-up mechanism in the Server Administration Guide.

Deprecated features

The following sections provide details on deprecated features.

Organization Redirect URL

The organization Redirect URL field is now deprecated and will be removed in a future release. When sending invitations, prefer selecting a client to control where users are redirected. Create a client with its base URL set to your desired redirect destination and pass its client_id when creating invitations.

Organization invitation endpoints without client_id

The invite-user and invite-existing-user admin client overloads that do not accept a client_id parameter are now deprecated. Use the overloads that accept an explicit client_id instead. When omitted, the endpoints default to the account client, but callers should migrate to always specify a client.

End of Life features

End of Life features have been previously deprecated and are removed in this release.

Deprecated cache stacks no longer supported

The following cache transport stacks are no longer supported:

  • azure

  • ec2

  • google

  • jdbc-ping-udp

  • kubernetes

  • tcp

  • udp

The only supported transport stack is jdbc-ping, which uses TCP for transport and the database for node discovery. Communication between nodes is encrypted using TLS by default.

Remove the cache-stack setting from your configuration to use the default jdbc-ping stack. If your cache configuration XML explicitly selects another transport stack, remove that selection as well. Remove any discovery settings specific to the previous stack, such as jgroups.dns.query.

Migrating to 26.8.1

Breaking changes

Breaking changes are identified as those that might require changes for existing users to their configurations or applications. In minor or patch releases, Keycloak will only introduce breaking changes to fix bugs.

SMTP StartTLS is required when enabled (CVE-2026-107121)

Previously, when a realm SMTP configuration had Enable StartTLS enabled, Keycloak used opportunistic STARTTLS. If the SMTP server did not advertise STARTTLS, or a network attacker stripped the advertisement, Keycloak continued and sent credentials and message content in plaintext.

Starting with this release, when StartTLS is enabled, Keycloak requires a successful STARTTLS upgrade before authentication or message data is sent. If the SMTP server does not support STARTTLS, sending email fails instead of falling back to plaintext.

If email delivery fails after upgrading, verify that your SMTP server supports STARTTLS, or disable Enable StartTLS if you intentionally use plaintext SMTP without TLS.

X.509 authenticators process key usage and extended key usage validations correctly

The X.509 user authenticators (X509/Validate Username Form for browser authentication and X509/Validate Username for direct access grants) include two optional configuration settings for key usage and extended key usage. These settings allow administrators to validate the presence of specific purposes within the corresponding certificate extensions. Previously, validation only occurred if the extension was marked as critical in the certificate. Starting with this version, validation is performed regardless of the critical status.

Although the previous behavior was considered a bug, a new provider option is available for each authenticator to revert to the legacy validation if necessary.

bin/kc.[sh|bat] start --spi-authenticator--auth-x509-client-username-form--legacy-critical-behavior true \
  --spi-authenticator--direct-grant-auth-x509-username--legacy-critical-behavior true

See X.509 client certificate user authentication for more information.

X.509 authenticators load CRL files only from the conf directory

Keycloak now loads a CRL file for X.509 client certificate authentication only when the file path resolves to a location inside the conf directory of the Keycloak installation. This applies to the CRL Path option and to CRL distribution points that contain a file path.

If a CRL file is stored outside the conf directory, move or mount the file into the conf directory and set the CRL Path option to the file path relative to that directory.

Limiting the size of CRL and OCSP responses for X.509 authenticators

Previously, Keycloak applied a general limit to the size of responses received from external HTTP servers. Starting with this release, the same limit also applies when X.509 authenticators load CRLs over HTTP or communicate with OCSP servers. By default, responses are limited to 10 MB to help prevent denial-of-service attacks.

You can use the following provider option to increase or decrease the limit.

Restricting the consumed responses to 1 MB
bin/kc.[sh|bat] --spi-connections-http-client-default-max-consumed-response-size=1000000

Notable changes

Notable changes may include internal behavior changes that prevent common misconfigurations, bugs that are fixed, or changes to simplify running Keycloak. It also lists significant changes to internal APIs.

Kerberos username/password authenticator performs local service-ticket round trip

Previously, the Kerberos provider authenticated the user only against the KDC server by performing an AS-REQ/AS-REP exchange. Consequently, authentication could succeed against an unintended or rogue KDC if it was inadvertently selected. This applied only to direct Kerberos username and password authentication, never to browser-based SPNEGO/negotiate authentication.

Starting with this release, after authenticating the user, Keycloak performs an additional service-ticket round trip for its configured service principal. A service ticket is requested using TGS-REQ/TGS-REP and validated with the configured local keytab.

Although no issues are expected, the previous behavior can be restored with the following provider options for the LDAP and Kerberos standalone providers:

bin/kc.[sh|bat] start --spi-storage--ldap--disable-kerberos-authentication-round-trip true \
  --spi-storage--kerberos--disable-kerberos-authentication-round-trip true
Registration access tokens are rejected by client registration endpoints of a different protocol

Updating or deleting a client through a client registration endpoint whose protocol does not match the client, such as using a SAML client’s registration access token against the openid-connect endpoint, is now rejected with 400 invalid_client, matching the behavior that already applied to read requests. Use the protocol-agnostic clients-registrations/default endpoint or the Admin REST API to manage clients of other protocols.

Conditional OTP authenticator now requires a trusted proxy for header-based rules

The Skip OTP for Header (SKIP_OTP_FOR_HTTP_HEADER) option in the Conditional OTP authenticator now verifies both that Keycloak is configured in reverse proxy mode (via --proxy-headers) and that the request arrived through a trusted proxy. If proxy headers are not configured, or if the proxy is not trusted, the skip-header rule is ignored and OTP is not skipped.

The Force OTP for Header (FORCE_OTP_FOR_HTTP_HEADER) option is not affected by this change because forcing OTP is always the safer direction.

Both options should only be used when Keycloak is configured in reverse proxy mode with headers correctly overwritten by the proxy. Without this, any client can inject a matching header value in their request and influence the OTP decision. See Header filtering recommendations for more details.

Semicolons are no longer treated as query parameter delimiters

Previously Keycloak treated the semicolon (;) as a query parameter delimiter in addition to the ampersand (&), so a request such as /path?a=1;b=2 was parsed as two parameters. Because different layers of the server applied this rule inconsistently, the parameters used to process a request could differ from those used to verify its signature. Keycloak now treats the semicolon as a literal character, so the same request yields a single parameter. If any of your clients rely on the semicolon as a query parameter delimiter, update them to use the ampersand instead.

Migrating to 26.8.0

Breaking changes

Breaking changes are identified as those that might require changes for existing users to their configurations or applications. In minor or patch releases, Keycloak will only introduce breaking changes to fix bugs.

Client secret no longer returned for view-only client permissions

The Admin REST API endpoints for retrieving client representations (GET /admin/realms/{realm}/clients and GET /admin/realms/{realm}/clients/{id}) no longer include the client secret in the response when the caller only has view-clients permission. To view the client secret, the caller must have manage-clients permission.

Previously, any caller with the view-clients role could read client secrets from the API response, which posed a security risk as it allowed read-only administrators to access sensitive credentials. After upgrading, callers with only view-clients permission will see a masked placeholder () instead of the actual secret value.

If you have delegated administrators or automation that reads client secrets using the view-clients role, ensure they are granted the manage-clients realm admin role or equivalent manage permission on the Clients resource type.

initiating_idp logout parameter ignored by default

The initiating_idp query parameter on the OIDC RP-initiated logout endpoint is now ignored by default. This parameter suppressed the logout at the upstream identity provider for the referenced broker.

After upgrading, Keycloak always performs the upstream identity provider logout during a browser logout, regardless of the initiating_idp parameter. To handle logout initiated by an upstream identity provider, rely on the standard OIDC back-channel or front-channel logout mechanisms instead.

To temporarily restore the previous behavior, enable the allow-initiating-idp-logout-param server option for the OpenID Connect provider. This option is deprecated and will be removed in a future release.

X509 user authentication requires CA Subject DN

Following the changes made to the X509 client authenticator, in this release the user counterparts also require the Certificate Authority subject DN option. The new configuration attribute is multi-valued and identifies the trusted anchor CA (or CAs) for user certificates. The new option is now compulsory in the admin console. Additionally, the option Revalidate Client Certificate should always be enabled to get the root certificate for the chain and check its subject DN, so it is now deprecated and no longer presented in the console. Previous configurations still work to maintain backwards compatibility, but the new behavior will be enforced in the next major version.

Note that Keycloak’s trust store (or mTLS store) is shared across all realms. The new configuration allows specifying the correct CAs for user certificates and avoids interference with other unrelated CAs.

Revalidating certificates can be problematic in certain installations (for example if a proxy is configured for TLS termination and the certificates are passed to Keycloak using headers). The trust-store or mTLS store in Keycloak should always be configured correctly to validate the client certificate chain. Please see Using a reverse proxy and Configuring trusted certificates for mTLS, for more information if you are using a TLS termination proxy and X509 client authentication.

Disabled clients are no longer added to the token audience

A disabled or removed client is never added to the aud claim of a token, and its client roles are not present in the resource_access claim. Tokens are still issued, only without that client, so logins and refreshes keep succeeding. This change addresses CVE-2026-93999.

For more details, see Disabled clients and the audience in the Server Administration Guide.

Resource URI matching in Authorization Services normalizes matrix parameters, dot segments, percent-encoding, and query/fragment components

When matching an incoming request URI against a resource URI configured in Authorization Services (the policy enforcer and the UMA permission endpoint), the URI is now normalized before comparison:

  • Matrix parameters (for example ;jsessionid=...), including percent-encoded (%3B), are stripped.

  • Dot segments (for example /foo/../admin, /./admin), including percent-encoded (%2E%2E, %2e.), are resolved.

  • Percent-encoded slashes (%2F) are decoded, and any duplicate slashes this produces are collapsed.

  • A trailing slash is stripped.

  • The query string and fragment are dropped before matching, so a resource configured for /api/admin also matches, for example, /api/admin?foo=bar and /api/admin#section.

This prevents a mutated form of a protected resource’s URI from evading its configured policy and falling through to a more permissive resource, such as a catch-all /* resource.

Resources configured with a full absolute URI (a scheme and authority, for example https://my.domain/api/admin) are unaffected beyond their path component — the scheme and authority are preserved, and only the path is normalized.

If your Authorization Services configuration relies on distinguishing resources by matrix parameters, dot segments, trailing slashes, or query string/fragment content in the resource URI, review your resource and policy configuration, since these are now treated as equivalent to their normalized form.

Identity provider mappers no longer grant administrative roles by default (CVE-2026-12388)

Identity provider mappers can grant realm roles to brokered users or add them to groups. Previously, an administrator who only held the manage-identity-providers permission could configure such a mapper to grant an administrative role or to add users to a group that carries administrative roles, escalating their own privileges beyond what that permission is meant to allow.

Identity providers now have an allowAdminRoleMapping setting that controls whether their mappers may assign administrative roles, either directly or through group membership. The setting defaults to false, including for identity providers that already exist when you upgrade. While it is disabled, any mapper that would grant an administrative role or add a user to a group that carries administrative roles is blocked, both when the mapper is created or updated and when a user logs in through the broker. Only an administrator with the manage-realm permission can enable the setting.

After upgrading, if you rely on identity provider mappers to assign administrative roles or to add brokered users to groups that carry administrative roles, an administrator with the manage-realm permission must enable Allow granting admin roles via mappers on each affected identity provider. Note that this change does not remove existing role or group assignments that may have been previously granted by identity provider mappers. If such removal is needed it needs to be done explicitly.

Organization alias validation

Organization alias validation now also applies during realm import and creation through the organization provider. Aliases containing ASCII spaces, control characters, or backslashes, or consisting solely of *, are rejected. Whitespace-only aliases no longer default to the organization name; omitted, null, and empty aliases still do. Unicode characters and double quotes remain supported, and existing stored aliases are unchanged. Before importing an existing realm, check for rejected alias values and update any application references if aliases must change.

Redirect URI and web origin hostnames compared case-insensitively

Valid redirect URIs and web origins now compare the URI scheme and hostname case-insensitively. This follows RFC 3986, which defines hostnames as case-insensitive, and replaces the exact string matching for scheme and hostname introduced in Keycloak 23.0.2.

For redirect URIs, path, query, fragment, and user-info remain case-sensitive. For example, a client configured with https://Example.COM/callback now accepts https://example.com/callback and HTTPS://example.com/callback, but still rejects https://example.com/Callback.

Web origins used for CORS and login-status iframe checks compare only scheme, host, and port (case-insensitively for scheme and host). Origins do not include path, query, fragment, or user-info.

Realms that previously listed multiple redirect URIs that differed only by hostname or scheme case can keep a single entry.

JavaScript Admin Client lookups declare null for a missing resource

The JavaScript Admin Client methods that look up a single resource and tolerate a 404, such as users.findOne(), clients.findOne(), groups.findOne() and roles.findOneById(), now declare null instead of undefined as their result when the resource does not exist. The methods already returned null at runtime, so only the TypeScript types change.

If your TypeScript code assigns these results to a variable typed as T | undefined, it no longer compiles. Change the type to T | null, or convert the result with ?? undefined, and check for null where the code checked for undefined.

Some methods that tolerate a 404 did not declare it at all, and now do:

  • roles.findUsersWithRole(), clients.findProtocolMappersByProtocol(), clientScopes.findProtocolMappersByProtocol() and workflows.scheduled() return an array or null.

  • authenticationManagement.getRequiredActionForAlias() returns RequiredActionProviderRepresentation | null instead of any.

  • clients.findOnePolicy() and clients.findOnePolicyWithType() return PolicyRepresentation | null instead of void.

Code that uses these results without checking for null no longer compiles.

Notable changes

Secrets referenced by OIDC Client CRs now require a label

Kubernetes Secrets referenced via spec.client.auth.secretRef in a KeycloakOIDCClient CR must now carry the label operator.keycloak.org/kind: KeycloakOIDCClient. Without this label the operator treats the Secret as missing and reports a HasErrors status condition on the CR, even if the Secret exists.

This change prevents arbitrary Secrets in the namespace from being read by the operator.

If you already have Secrets used by OIDC Client CRs, add the required label before upgrading:

kubectl label secret <secret-name> operator.keycloak.org/kind=KeycloakOIDCClient
Refresh token expiration now reflects the session idle timeout

When Offline Session Max Limited is disabled (the default), offline refresh tokens previously had no exp claim and the token response returned refresh_expires_in: 0. The server still enforced the Offline Session Idle timeout and rejected refresh attempts after that window with invalid_grant. For all refresh tokens, the exp claim and refresh_expires_in were previously based only on the client session idle and max lifespan timeouts, without considering the user session idle timeout.

Offline refresh tokens now always include an exp claim. For all refresh tokens (online and offline), the expiration is now capped by the user session idle timeout, so refresh_expires_in reflects the minimum of the session idle timeout and any client-specific session idle timeout. This allows clients to proactively refresh before the idle window closes instead of encountering unexpected invalid_grant errors.

NO_PROXY entries with leading dots or spaces after commas now parsed correctly

NO_PROXY entries with a leading dot (for example, .corp.com) or whitespace after a comma (for example, localhost, internal.corp.com) previously failed to match silently, causing traffic to go through the proxy despite the exclusion rule. Both patterns are now handled correctly.

If your deployment relied on this broken parsing and routed traffic through the proxy intentionally by using a leading-dot entry as a no-op, that traffic will now bypass the proxy after upgrading.

kcadm and kcreg now enforce owner-only permissions on existing configuration files

The kcadm and kcreg CLI tools now set owner-only file permissions (0600) on their configuration files every time credentials are written, not only when the file is first created. Previously, if a configuration file already existed with broader permissions (for example, 0644), the tools would store access tokens and other sensitive credentials without restricting the file permissions, which posed a security risk as it allowed other users on the same system to read the stored tokens.

After upgrading, any pre-existing configuration file with group- or world-readable permissions will be automatically restricted to owner-only access the next time kcadm config credentials or kcreg config credentials is executed. No manual action is required.

Login failures now stored in the database by default

Login failure data for brute force detection is now stored in the database by default, instead of only in the embedded Infinispan loginFailures cache. This ensures that temporarily locked-out users remain locked out across cluster restarts, and reduces memory consumption from accumulated login failure entries.

This change is controlled by the new login-failures versioned feature. The previous in-memory behavior (login-failures:v1) is deprecated. To restore it, set --features=login-failures:v1. Note that login-failures:v1 is incompatible with the stateless feature.

Deployments using the multi-site or clusterless features continue to store login failure data in the external Infinispan cluster, regardless of the login-failures feature version.

As a trade-off, users may observe increased database connection usage and higher CPU usage on the database.

The option spi-brute-force-protector-default-brute-force-detector-allow-concurrent-requests is deprecated and has no effect with login-failures:v2. Instead, concurrent logins for the same user are executed serially for each node.

Stateless feature promoted from preview to supported

The stateless feature has been promoted from preview to supported. It is disabled by default and can be enabled with --features=stateless.

If you previously relied on --features=preview to enable the stateless feature, you must now enable it explicitly with --features=stateless, as --features=preview no longer activates supported features.

Clusterless feature will be removed

The experimental clusterless feature will be removed in a future release. The stateless feature is the successor for running Keycloak without depending on an external Infinispan cluster. While clusterless offloads all session data to an external Infinispan cluster, stateless stores session data in the database and uses the embedded Infinispan only as a cache.

If you are using --features=clusterless, migrate to --features=stateless before upgrading to a future release.

AuthZEN changes

The experimental AuthZEN endpoints now require the caller to authenticate with a confidential client’s access token. Previously, a user’s access token was also accepted.

Client session listings filter sessions by user visibility

The Admin REST API endpoints for listing client sessions (GET /admin/realms/{realm}/clients/{id}/user-sessions and GET /admin/realms/{realm}/clients/{id}/offline-sessions) now filter out sessions belonging to users that the caller does not have permission to view. Previously, any caller with the view-clients role could see all user sessions for a client, potentially exposing user identities to callers without the view-users role. After upgrading, callers without view-users permission will see fewer results from these endpoints.

Administrators that can map a role can also view it

An administrator that is allowed to map a role to a user is now also allowed to view that role. This applies to the manage-users realm admin role, to the map-role and map-roles permissions of fine-grained admin permissions V1, and to the map-role operation of the Roles resource type in fine-grained admin permissions V2.

Previously, viewing a realm role additionally required the view-realm role, or a view permission on the client for a client role. As a result, the role mappings of a user or group returned by GET /admin/realms/{realm}/users/{id}/role-mappings and GET /admin/realms/{realm}/groups/{id}/role-mappings omitted the roles an administrator could map but not view, the client-level endpoints GET /admin/realms/{realm}/users/{id}/role-mappings/clients/{client} and GET /admin/realms/{realm}/groups/{id}/role-mappings/clients/{client} returned 403 Forbidden without a view permission on the client, and the realmRoles and clientRoles of a group returned by GET /admin/realms/{realm}/groups/{id} were filtered by the same rule.

Now all these endpoints return the roles the administrator is allowed to view or map, and filter out the remaining roles instead of returning 403 Forbidden.

After upgrading, these administrators can also read the role through GET /admin/realms/{realm}/roles-by-id/{id}, including its attributes and composites. Reading the management permission references of a role with fine-grained admin permissions V1 still requires permission to view the realm or the client the role belongs to. Roles that an administrator can neither view nor map remain hidden. The only difference for these administrators is that the client-level role mapping endpoints now return an empty list instead of 403 Forbidden when they have no view permission on the client.

Client Secret Rotation is now enabled by default

The client-secret-rotation feature has been promoted from preview to supported and is now enabled by default. If you previously enabled this feature explicitly with --features=client-secret-rotation or --features=preview, that configuration is no longer required.

SCIM API is now enabled by default

The scim-api feature has been promoted from preview to supported and is now enabled by default. If you previously enabled this feature explicitly with --features=scim-api or --features=preview, that configuration is no longer required.

show-config now masks all SPI option values

The show-config command now masks all SPI option values. The command is not currently aware of provider factory configuration property metadata, so it cannot determine whether a property is marked as isSecret.

Redesigned identity provider buttons on the login page

The social identity provider section on the login page has been redesigned with updated icons and layout. For details, see the GitHub issue. Custom login themes that override social-providers.ftl or apply CSS targeting the identity provider icons may render incorrectly after upgrading.

Test your custom login theme to ensure the identity provider buttons display correctly.

Extension migration when using the Vert.x HTTP client

When the experimental Vert.x HTTP client is enabled (--features=http-client:v2), getHttpClient() returns a compatibility bridge that translates Apache API calls to Vert.x internally, so existing extensions continue to work without changes.

Extension providers should migrate to one of:

  • SimpleHttp.create(session) for general HTTP calls (GET, POST, PUT, DELETE with headers, parameters, and JSON/form bodies).

  • The provider-neutral helper methods on HttpClientProvider: getString(uri), getInputStream(uri), postText(uri, text).

These APIs work identically with both the Apache and Vert.x clients. For details, see the Configuring outgoing HTTP requests guide.

Token introspection act claim now uses user ID for sub

The token introspection endpoint previously set the act.sub field to the impersonator’s username. It now uses the impersonator’s user ID to be consistent with the act claim in JWT access tokens and ID tokens. The preferred_username field has been added to the introspection act object for consumers that need the impersonator’s username.

If you have resource servers that parse act.sub from introspection responses and expect a username, update them to read act.preferred_username instead.

Group policy matching for bare group names (CVE-2026-19608)

Group-based policies in Authorization Services use the groups claim from the token as the first source of truth. When the claim is not defined or empty, the policy falls back to the user’s actual group memberships from the realm. Bare group names in token claims are now resolved to top-level realm groups only. Previously, a bare name like Admins in a group claim would match any group named Admins regardless of its position in the group hierarchy. This allowed users who are members of one group to satisfy policies targeting a different group with the same name at a different path.

If your deployment uses the Group Membership protocol mapper with Full group path disabled and relies on group policies targeting nested groups, enable Full group path in the protocol mapper configuration so that tokens include the complete group path (for example, /Organization/Admins instead of Admins).

Group names that contain slashes (for example, a group named /Organization/Admins) are now resolved as top-level group names first, before being treated as paths. This prevents a group name from being confused with a nested group path.

Group policies that use full group paths in claims are not affected unless a top-level group name is identical to a nested group path. In that ambiguous case, the top-level group name takes precedence.

Token exchange delegation now requires FGAP V2 permissions

The experimental token exchange delegation feature no longer uses the impersonation role from the realm-management client to authorize delegation. Instead, delegation is controlled exclusively through Fine-grained admin permissions version 2 (FGAP V2) using the new delegate scope on the Users resource type and the delegate-members scope on the Groups resource type.

If you were previously using delegation with the impersonation role, you need to:

  1. Enable Admin Permissions (FGAP V2) on your realm.

  2. Create a User permission with the delegate scope and assign a policy targeting the administrators or service accounts that should be allowed to delegate. Alternatively, create a Group permission with the delegate-members scope to grant delegation for members of a specific group.

FGAP V1 does not support delegation permissions.

Token exchange delegation scope renamed from delegation to delegation:user

The parameterized client scope used by the experimental token exchange delegation feature has been renamed from delegation to delegation:user to better reflect that this scope targets delegation to a specific user (administrator).

The full scope format in token requests changes from delegation:<administrator> to delegation:user:<administrator>. For example, a scope value that was previously delegation:admin1 is now delegation:user:admin1.

During migration, the delegation:user and delegation:client client scopes are automatically created in existing realms as Optional client scopes. Delegation also requires configuring a delegation permission through FGAP V2. If you had a delegation client scope from a previous version, it can be safely removed after migration. Update clients and automation to request delegation:user:<administrator>.

Email is no longer marked as verified by other flows

Previously, some flows marked the email of a user as verified even though their purpose was not email verification. This could make an account registered by a different party than the owner of the email address look trusted. An email is now marked as verified only when the user completes an email verification. This changes the behavior of identity provider account linking by email, of admin-initiated action emails that do not include the Verify Email action, and of the verifiable credential offer emails of the OID4VCI feature. The Trust Email setting of identity providers and LDAP federation is not affected, as it is an explicit decision of the administrator to trust the email from the external source.

Offline session expiration indexes rebuilt with session bucket and coarse refresh columns

The OFFLINE_USER_SESSION table has a new LAST_SESSION_REFRESH_COARSE column and updated indexes IDX_USER_SESSION_EXPIRATION_CREATED and IDX_USER_SESSION_EXPIRATION_LAST_REFRESH. On PostgreSQL and Microsoft SQL Server, IDX_OFFLINE_USS_BY_BROKER_SESSION_ID is also rebuilt as a filtered index. If the table contains more than 300000 entries, Keycloak will skip the index creation by default during the automatic schema migration and instead log the SQL statement on the console during migration to be applied manually after Keycloak’s startup. See the Upgrading Guide for details on how to configure a different limit.

Automatic non-blocking index creation after startup

When Keycloak skips creating an index during schema migration because the table exceeds the row count threshold, the index checker now automatically creates the missing index in the background after startup on databases that support non-blocking index creation. This applies to PostgreSQL (CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY), Oracle (CREATE INDEX …​ ONLINE), MySQL/MariaDB (online DDL by default), and Microsoft SQL Server Enterprise, Developer, and Azure SQL editions (CREATE INDEX …​ WITH (ONLINE = ON)).

On PostgreSQL, if an invalid index exists from a previously failed CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY, Keycloak detects it, drops it, and recreates it automatically.

On databases that do not support non-blocking index creation, or when the migration strategy is set to manual or validate, Keycloak continues to log the SQL statement for manual execution without creating the index automatically.

Disabling caching of persistent user sessions

Caching of persistent user sessions in the embedded Infinispan can be disabled. Recent optimizations to the database layer, including async commits, improved indexes, and cheaper refresh-date updates, make persistent sessions without caching a viable option. Disabling the cache reduces memory usage and network traffic between nodes, enabling smoother rolling updates.

Users may observe increased database connection usage and higher CPU usage on the database if they are using token introspection or token exchange extensively. For users already using persistent sessions, the database’s own caching layer should prevent any additional IOPS.

To disable caching, set --spi-user-sessions--infinispan--use-caches=false.

Certificate Common Name truncated to 64 characters

When Keycloak generates self-signed certificates (for example, during SAML client creation), the Common Name (CN) is now truncated to 64 characters to comply with the RFC 5280 ub-common-name upper bound. Previously, values longer than 64 characters, such as long client IDs, were used as-is. With the BouncyCastle 1.85 update, this caused certificate generation to fail.

If you have tooling that inspects the CN of Keycloak-generated certificates and expects it to match a value longer than 64 characters, be aware that the CN may now be shorter.

Transaction isolation level changed to READ COMMITTED for MySQL and MariaDB in stateless mode

When the stateless feature is enabled, Keycloak now sets the transaction isolation level to READ COMMITTED for MySQL and MariaDB. This prevents deadlocks that occur under concurrent logins. If binary logging is enabled with binlog_format = STATEMENT, this change will cause write operations to fail. Keycloak logs an error at startup when it detects this incompatible setting.

See the Transaction isolation level in stateless mode section in the database configuration guide for details on binlog_format requirements.

Changing the default transaction isolation level for all modes is being considered for a future release.

Galera Cluster considerations
Warning
 Galera Cluster is not a supported database configuration for Keycloak. The guidance in this section is provided on a best-effort basis. Before reporting bugs, reproduce them with a supported database configuration first.

On a multi-writer Galera setup, concurrent writes to the same row on different nodes can trigger certification failures, and asynchronous replication can cause stale reads. Keycloak logs an error at startup when it detects Galera with wsrep_sync_wait = 0.

See the Galera Cluster section in the database configuration guide for recommended settings and limitations.

PostgreSQL connection timeout with the AWS JDBC Wrapper

The connectTimeout property is now applied when using the AWS JDBC Wrapper for Amazon Aurora PostgreSQL, as the wrapper passes this property through to the underlying PostgreSQL driver. Previously, it was skipped for all custom JDBC drivers. The logServerErrorDetail property is now also applied when using the AWS JDBC Wrapper, and skipped for other custom JDBC drivers. The targetServerType property remains excluded when using the AWS JDBC Wrapper, as the wrapper has its own failover routing mechanism.

Asynchronous commit on MS SQL Server and Oracle databases

The asynchronous commit optimization, previously available only on PostgreSQL, now also supports MS SQL Server and Oracle databases. Transactions that update only ephemeral tables like persisted user sessions, client sessions, login failures, or events will use delayed/asynchronous commit, improving throughput. Logouts will still force a synchronous commit.

On MS SQL Server, the DBA must enable delayed durability first: ALTER DATABASE …​ SET DELAYED_DURABILITY = ALLOWED. On Oracle, no database-level configuration is required. On both databases, the optimization is automatically disabled when using XA datasources (--transaction-xa-enabled=true).

You can opt out of this feature by setting --spi-connections-jpa--quarkus--async-commit=false.

Stateless feature requires a distinct cluster name

The stateless feature now requires --cache-embedded-cluster-name to be set explicitly to a value other than the default ISPN. Keycloak refuses to start if the stateless feature is enabled and this option is missing or still set to ISPN.

Previously, the cluster name had to be configured through the low-level SPI option spi-cache-embedded--default--cluster-name (or the environment variable KC_SPI_CACHE_EMBEDDED__DEFAULT__CLUSTER_NAME). This option is now superseded by the first-class CLI option --cache-embedded-cluster-name (environment variable KC_CACHE_EMBEDDED_CLUSTER_NAME).

If you use the stateless feature, add --cache-embedded-cluster-name=<unique-name> to your startup command with a name that is unique per deployment. Two deployments sharing the same database must use different cluster names so that cross-cluster cache invalidation works correctly.

First-class CLI options for cluster and node name

The embedded cache cluster name and node name are now configurable with the first-class CLI options --cache-embedded-cluster-name (environment variable KC_CACHE_EMBEDDED_CLUSTER_NAME) and --cache-embedded-node-name (environment variable KC_CACHE_EMBEDDED_NODE_NAME). These replace the low-level SPI options spi-cache-embedded--default--cluster-name and spi-cache-embedded--default--node-name. The SPI options continue to work but the new CLI options are recommended.

The node name appears in Infinispan and JGroups log messages, metrics tags, and management tools. By default, a random name is generated on each start, which makes it difficult to correlate metrics and logs across restarts. Setting a stable node name improves observability, especially with tools such as Grafana dashboards and log aggregation.

Keycloak Operator

When deploying with the Keycloak Operator, this is now set automatically to the Kubernetes Pod name. No additional configuration is needed.

Standalone Kubernetes deployments (without the Operator)

Add the following environment variable to your pod spec to set the node name to the Pod name:

env:
  - name: KC_CACHE_EMBEDDED_NODE_NAME
    valueFrom:
      fieldRef:
        fieldPath: metadata.name

Bare-metal or VM deployments

Pass --cache-embedded-node-name=<name> with a value that uniquely identifies each node, for example the hostname.

Validating cluster names when using jdbc-ping discovery

Keycloak nodes that use the jdbc-ping cluster stack periodically check whether other Keycloak deployments with a different cluster name are connecting to the same database. If this is detected and the stateless feature is not enabled, each node logs an error and reports itself as unhealthy via the health endpoint.

The stateless feature provides the cross-cluster cache invalidation mechanism that makes it safe for separate Keycloak deployments to share a database. Without it, realm and authorization data cached by one deployment is never invalidated when the other deployment makes a change, which leads to stale caches and incorrect behavior.

Impact on blue-green deployments

A standard blue-green upgrade keeps only one cluster active at a time: the old cluster is fully shut down before the new one starts receiving traffic. With this strategy, both clusters never run simultaneously, so no second cluster name is ever visible in the database and this check does not trigger.

If your blue-green process involves a brief overlap period where both clusters are up concurrently — for example, to pre-warm caches — and you use distinct cluster names without the stateless feature, both deployments will log errors and report unhealthy during that window. This is intentional: without the stateless feature, neither deployment invalidates the other’s caches, which is unsafe when both are serving traffic at the same time.

Recommendations

  • Single cluster: no action required. The check does not trigger.

  • Blue-green with only one cluster active at a time: no action required. The check does not trigger because both clusters are never running simultaneously.

  • Blue-green with a concurrent overlap period and distinct cluster names, without stateless: the unhealthy status during the overlap is expected and correct. Either accept the brief unhealthy window, avoid running both clusters concurrently, or enable the stateless feature.

  • Multiple independent deployments sharing the same database: enable the stateless feature and assign each deployment a unique cluster name via --cache-embedded-cluster-name. Without stateless, cross-deployment cache invalidation does not work and serving traffic from multiple deployments simultaneously leads to incorrect behavior.

Quarkus upgraded from 3.33 to 3.40

Keycloak now runs on Quarkus 3.40 (up from 3.33 in 26.7.0). This upgrade brings internal improvements across the Quarkus platform. While no user-facing configuration changes are required, custom extensions or providers that depend on Quarkus-internal APIs should be retested.

Organization identity providers now support many-to-many linking

The relationship between identity providers and organizations has changed from many-to-one to many-to-many. Previously, an organization could have multiple identity providers, but each identity provider could only be linked to a single organization. A single identity provider can now be linked to multiple organizations, with independent per-association configuration for auto-membership and membership type.

During migration, the following changes are applied automatically:

  • Existing identity provider-to-organization links are migrated to the new ORG_IDENTITY_PROVIDER join table with AUTO_MEMBERSHIP=true and MEMBERSHIP_TYPE=MANAGED, preserving the previous default behavior. New links created after the upgrade default to MEMBERSHIP_TYPE=UNMANAGED.

  • The ORGANIZATION_ID column is removed from the IDENTITY_PROVIDER table.

  • The ORG_DOMAIN table is extended with new columns for domain routing: REALM_ID, IDP_ID (identity provider routing), and AUTO_REDIRECT. Each domain is now a first-class entity with its own routing configuration.

  • Three identity provider configuration entries are migrated to the new ORG_DOMAIN columns and then removed:

    • kc.org.domain — migrated to ORG_DOMAIN.IDP_ID (identity provider routing). If the value was ANY, all domains of the organization receive the identity provider routing, except those listed in the exclusion list below.

    • kc.org.broker.redirect.mode.email-matches — migrated to ORG_DOMAIN.AUTO_REDIRECT

    • kc.org.excluded.domains — excluded domains are migrated with IDP_ID=NULL (no routing)

      The concept of domain exclusions no longer exists as a separate setting. Instead, each domain now has its own identity provider routing configuration. A domain that previously appeared in the exclusion list is migrated with no identity provider assigned (IDP_ID=NULL), which means users with that email domain see the standard login form instead of being redirected.
