To achieve the same effect going forward, edit the domain in the organization’s Domains tab and leave the Identity provider field empty. Conversely, to start routing a previously excluded domain, select the desired identity provider from the dropdown.

  • Several display settings previously configured on the organization’s identity provider link dialog have moved to the identity provider’s own Settings page at the realm level, under the Organization settings section:

    • Hide on login page when organization is not resolved

    • Show on login page for unlinked members

  • Identity provider mappers that target organization groups now store the target organization ID explicitly in the mapper configuration (orgId field). Previously, the organization was inferred implicitly, which only worked when the identity provider was linked to a single organization. During migration, existing mappers that target organization groups are automatically backfilled with the appropriate organization ID. After upgrading, if your identity provider is now linked to multiple organizations, create separate mappers for each organization’s groups.

After upgrading, verify the domain routing and per-association configuration in the admin console:

  1. Navigate to each organization’s Domains tab to confirm identity provider routing and auto-redirect settings.

  2. Navigate to each organization’s Identity providers tab to confirm auto-membership and membership type.

  3. If you use the Admin REST API to manage identity provider-organization links, update your integrations. The organizationId field on IdentityProviderRepresentation has been replaced by a list of organization links accessible through the organization identity provider endpoints.

For details on the new configuration model, see Managing identity providers and Managing organization domains in the Server Administration Guide.

Default JPA persistence unit renamed to <default>

Keycloak’s default JPA persistence unit is now configured through Quarkus Hibernate ORM properties instead of an internal persistence.xml. As a result, the default persistence unit, previously named keycloak-default, is now Quarkus' default (unnamed) persistence unit, reported as <default>.

If a custom provider archive defines multiple persistence units within a single persistence.xml and an entity-less unit should load META-INF/orm.xml, explicitly declare the standard <mapping-file>META-INF/orm.xml</mapping-file> element in that <persistence-unit> to ensure its entities are properly bound and to prevent cross-unit mapping collisions.

New executor to secure client cluster node registration

A new client policy executor, secure-client-node-hostname, is available to address server-side request forgery (SSRF) via the legacy adapter cluster node registration endpoint (/clients-managements/register-node). Without this executor, a confidential client could register an attacker-chosen hostname that Keycloak would later use as the destination for management callbacks such as logout propagation and push-revocation.

This protection is opt-in and is not active after upgrading unless explicitly configured.

If your deployment uses the legacy adapter node registration feature (Wildfly/JBoss adapter or any application that calls register-node directly), add the secure-client-node-hostname executor to a client policy and configure at least one allowed hostname pattern. Node registrations that do not match any pattern are rejected.

Hostnames must be supplied as a DNS name or IP address without a port number. Port suffixes (for example, app.example.com:8443) are always rejected regardless of the configured patterns.

If you plan to enable this executor, audit the registeredNodes map for any affected clients before activating the policy. Any entry that contains a port suffix will no longer be accepted at registration time. Follow these steps in order:

  1. Delete each existing registeredNodes entry that would not satisfy the intended allowlist, using the admin API endpoint DELETE /admin/realms/{realm}/clients/{id}/nodes/{node} or via the admin console.

  2. Enable the policy with the secure-client-node-hostname executor added.

  3. Register each replacement hostname through POST /admin/realms/{realm}/clients/{id}/nodes so that the new entry is validated against the active policy.

Re-registering a replacement hostname without first deleting the old entry leaves the old key in the map, which Keycloak continues using for management callbacks regardless of the active policy.

Deprecated features

The following sections provide details on deprecated features.

Client registration providers default, install, and saml2-entity-descriptor

The following client registration endpoints are now deprecated and will be removed in a future major release:

  • /realms/<realm>/clients-registrations/default — Keycloak Client Representation provider

  • /realms/<realm>/clients-registrations/install/<clientId> — Adapter Installation Configuration provider

  • /realms/<realm>/clients-registrations/saml2-entity-descriptor — SAML Entity Descriptor provider

These endpoints are not backed by any specification. For default, use either the Admin REST API or the standards-compliant OpenID Connect dynamic client registration endpoint (/realms/<realm>/clients-registrations/openid-connect). For install, retrieve adapter configuration with GET /admin/realms/<realm>/clients/<client-uuid>/installation/providers/<provider-id>. For saml2-entity-descriptor, first convert the descriptor with POST /admin/realms/<realm>/client-description-converter, then create the returned client representation with POST /admin/realms/<realm>/clients.

A WARN-level log message is now emitted whenever these endpoints are invoked.

Multi-cluster v1 (multi-site feature) is deprecated

The multi-site feature, used for multi-cluster v1 deployments with an external Infinispan cluster, is deprecated and will be removed in a future major release. The stateless feature (multi-cluster v2) is the recommended replacement. It stores session data in the database, eliminating the need for an external Infinispan cluster, its cross-site replication, and the associated fencing automation.

If you are using --features=multi-site, migrate to --features=stateless before upgrading to a future release. For detailed migration instructions, see Migrating from multi-cluster v1 to v2 in the High Availability Guide.

Client certificate REST API endpoints for key pair management

Several REST API endpoints under /admin/realms/{realm}/clients/{id}/certificates/{attr} that deal with server-side key pair generation or private key storage are now deprecated:

  • POST …​/generate — Generates a key pair on the server. Clients should generate their own key pairs locally and upload only the public certificate.

  • POST …​/upload — Superseded by POST …​/upload-certificate, which is functionally identical but not deprecated.

  • POST …​/download — Downloads a keystore containing the client’s private key. Clients should manage their own private keys.

  • POST …​/generate-and-download — Generates a key pair and returns the private key as a keystore download. Clients should generate their own key pairs locally.

These endpoints no longer store private keys in the database. The generate endpoint returns the private key in the response but only persists the public certificate. The generate-and-download endpoint returns the private key in the keystore download but only persists the public certificate.

Use the POST …​/upload-certificate endpoint to upload a public certificate or JWKS after generating your key pair locally.

Introspection endpoint 'jwt' extension

The proprietary Keycloak extension for invoking the introspection endpoint with the HTTP header Accept: application/jwt instead of Accept: application/json is now deprecated. If you have a lightweight access token that you want to exchange for a full access token, use Token Exchange instead.

'Full scope allowed' switch on clients is deprecated

The Full Scope Allowed switch on OIDC clients is deprecated and will be removed in a future release.

When Full Scope Allowed is enabled (which is the default for new clients), every access token issued for that client contains all roles the authenticated user holds across all clients and the realm. This is convenient during early development, but is a security risk in production: a single compromised token grants access to every resource the user can reach. The correct approach is to disable Full Scope Allowed and explicitly configure role scope mappings so that each client’s tokens contain only the roles that client actually needs.

Deprecation warning in the server log

Starting with this release, Keycloak logs a WARN-level message at token-issuance time for every client that still has Full Scope Allowed enabled. The warning is suppressed for the built-in admin clients security-admin-console and admin-cli (see below).

To silence the warning for all other clients temporarily, disable logging for the category org.keycloak.protocol.oidc.endpoints.TokenEndpoint.full-scope-allowed. However, the recommended action is to keep the warning enabled and instead migrate the clients that produce it, so that your deployment is prepared for the full removal of the switch in a future release.

Built-in admin clients

The built-in clients security-admin-console and admin-cli currently have Full Scope Allowed enabled and the deprecation warning is intentionally suppressed for them. The Keycloak team will fix these clients in an upcoming release as part of the planned migration path.

It is not strictly needed to manually disable Full Scope Allowed for these clients in your own environment. Disabling it manually can break Admin Console login immediately, and may require additional manual migration steps in future Keycloak upgrades. If you prefer to disable it for the built-in clients, you may need additional steps, for example, create a client scope with the appropriate role scopes and add that client scope to the built-in admin clients. Keycloak will provide a migration path for both cases (environments where built-in admin clients still have Full Scope Allowed enabled and environments where it has already been disabled).

How to migrate your own clients

  1. For each client that triggers the deprecation warning in the log, open the client in the Admin Console, navigate to Client scopes → the dedicated client scope → Scope, and disable Full Scope Allowed.

  2. Explicitly add the role scope mappings that the client requires under the same Scope tab or on the linked client scopes.

  3. Use the Client scopes → Evaluate tool (See details here) to verify that the resulting token contains exactly the roles the client needs.

To enforce that all newly created or updated clients in a realm have Full Scope Allowed disabled automatically, configure a client policy with the full-scope-disabled executor. See Enforcing Full Scope Allowed to be disabled in the Server Administration Guide for step-by-step instructions, including how to exclude built-in clients from the policy.

Client switches in the 'OpenID Connect Compatibility Modes'

Several client switches in the admin console under OpenID Connect Compatibility Modes were introduced for backwards compatibility with older OIDC/OAuth2 client adapters. These switches are now deprecated and will be removed in a future version. Some were deprecated in previous versions. The newly deprecated switches are:

  • Exclude Session State From Authentication Response

  • Exclude Issuer From Authentication Response

  • Use lower-case bearer type in token responses

Update your client applications if you still rely on these switches, as they will be removed in a future version.

Option Revalidate Client Certificate is deprecated for the X509 user authenticators

As described in the breaking changes section, the option Revalidate Client Certificate is deprecated for the X509 user authenticators. This option was used to revalidate the certificate chain presented by the user against the server trust store. In the future, revalidation will always be performed and the trust store should be configured accordingly.

HttpResponse#getStatus() and setStatus(int) in server SPI

org.keycloak.http.HttpResponse#getStatus() and org.keycloak.http.HttpResponse#setStatus(int) are now deprecated. These methods are not useful for JAX-RS resource endpoints because the JAX-RS runtime usually determines the final HTTP status from the endpoint return value or exception. Use JAX-RS response handling for setting and observing status codes in resource endpoints.

Route in AUTH_SESSION_ID cookie for sticky sessions

Having the load balancer route appended to the AUTH_SESSION_ID cookie is now deprecated.

Since the optimizations in 26.6, sticky sessions no longer bring a significant benefit given the complex setup on the proxy side to get this working. Authentication sessions are created on the node where the browser starts the session, and while a session may move to another node on a subsequent request, the overhead is low. Most interactions such as backchannel calls and token refreshes do not use cookies and are distributed across nodes regardless. The recommendation is to aim for an even distribution of load across Keycloak nodes instead of using sticky sessions.

The options spi-sticky-session-encoder--infinispan--should-attach-route and spi-sticky-session-encoder--remote--should-attach-route will be removed in a future release.

The setting is still enabled by default to avoid breaking existing setups. To avoid the deprecation warning at startup, set those SPI options to false.

Disabling persistent user sessions (volatile sessions) is deprecated

Disabling the persistent-user-sessions feature (volatile sessions) is deprecated and will be removed in a future release. Persistent user sessions will become the only supported mode. If you have explicitly disabled persistent user sessions with --features-disabled=persistent-user-sessions, remove this option before upgrading to a future release.

In-memory login failures (login-failures:v1) is deprecated

The previous default of storing login failure data only in the in-memory Infinispan cache (login-failures:v1) is deprecated and will be removed in a future release. Login failures are now stored in the database by default (login-failures:v2). If you have explicitly enabled login-failures:v1, plan to remove this option before upgrading to a future release.

Use refresh tokens for client credentials grant switch on clients is deprecated

The Use refresh tokens for client credentials grant switch on clients is deprecated and will be removed in a future release. This switch was introduced solely for backwards compatibility and it is discouraged to use service accounts together with refresh tokens.

Instead of using a refresh token to obtain a new access token, clients should always send a new client credentials grant request with their client credentials. This approach avoids the overhead of managing refresh tokens for machine-to-machine flows and aligns with the recommended practices for OAuth 2.0 service accounts.

Kerberos credential delegation is deprecated

Kerberos credential delegation (the gss delegation credential protocol mapper) is now deprecated for security reasons and will be removed in a future release.

Keycloak Realm Operator reached end of life

The Keycloak Realm Operator has reached its end of life and its repository will be archived.

The Realm Operator was introduced as a temporary workaround to compensate mainly for the missing Client custom resources in the Keycloak Operator. Its KeycloakRealm and KeycloakClient custom resources are superseded by the following Keycloak Operator custom resources:

  • KeycloakRealmImport to create realm imports. For more details, see the Realm Import guide.

  • KeycloakOIDCClient and KeycloakSAMLClient, available as a preview feature, to manage OIDC and SAML clients declaratively. For more details, see the Managing Keycloak Clients guide.

Migrating to 26.7.5

Breaking changes

Breaking changes are identified as those that might require changes for existing users to their configurations or applications. In minor or patch releases, Keycloak will only introduce breaking changes to fix bugs.

Client policy source groups condition matches the full group path

The client-updater-source-groups client policy condition now matches groups by their full path instead of the simple group name. A configured value without a leading slash is treated as a top level group, so a condition configured with topGroup matches /topGroup and keeps behaving as before. Top level groups configured by their simple name do not require any change.

Only the conditions configured with the simple name of a subgroup need to be updated to the full path of that group, for example /topGroup/level2group.

OrganizationProvider#getByAlias no longer has a default implementation

org.keycloak.organization.OrganizationProvider#getByAlias(String) is now an abstract method. If you provide a custom OrganizationProvider, you must implement it.

The implementation must perform an unfiltered lookup of the organization with the given alias. The previous default implementation delegated to getAllStream, which applies fine-grained admin permission filters.

Consequently, getByAlias and getByMember(UserModel) are no longer subject to Fine-grained admin permissions, like getById(String) and getByDomainName(String). Administrative callers are expected to check access to the returned organization themselves.

Migrating to 26.7.4

Breaking changes

Breaking changes are identified as those that might require changes for existing users to their configurations or applications. In minor or patch releases, Keycloak will only introduce breaking changes to fix bugs.

Resource URI matching in Authorization Services normalizes matrix parameters, dot segments, percent-encoding, and query/fragment components

When matching an incoming request URI against a resource URI configured in Authorization Services (the policy enforcer and the UMA permission endpoint), the URI is now normalized before comparison:

  • Matrix parameters (for example ;jsessionid=...), including percent-encoded (%3B), are stripped.

  • Dot segments (for example /foo/../admin, /./admin), including percent-encoded (%2E%2E, %2e.), are resolved.

  • Percent-encoded slashes (%2F) are decoded, and any duplicate slashes this produces are collapsed.

  • A trailing slash is stripped.

  • The query string and fragment are dropped before matching, so a resource configured for /api/admin also matches, for example, /api/admin?foo=bar and /api/admin#section.

This prevents a mutated form of a protected resource’s URI from evading its configured policy and falling through to a more permissive resource, such as a catch-all /* resource.

Resources configured with a full absolute URI (a scheme and authority, for example https://my.domain/api/admin) are unaffected beyond their path component — the scheme and authority are preserved, and only the path is normalized.

If your Authorization Services configuration relies on distinguishing resources by matrix parameters, dot segments, trailing slashes, or query string/fragment content in the resource URI, review your resource and policy configuration, since these are now treated as equivalent to their normalized form.

Migrating to 26.7.3

Breaking changes

Breaking changes are identified as those that might require changes for existing users to their configurations or applications. In minor or patch releases, Keycloak will only introduce breaking changes to fix bugs.

Authorization services claim handling changes

Two changes affect how claims are handled in the authorization services token endpoint:

  • Claims with the kc. prefix are now reserved for server-controlled evaluation context attributes (such as kc.time.date_time, kc.client.id, and kc.realm.name). User-supplied claims with this prefix, whether pushed via claim_token or via the UMA permission ticket, are filtered out before policy evaluation. If your authorization policies rely on custom claims that start with kc., rename them to use a different prefix.

  • When both a UMA permission ticket and a claim_token contain claims with the same key, ticket claims now take precedence. Previously, claim_token claims would silently override ticket claims on collision. This change ensures that claims set by the resource server when creating the permission ticket cannot be overridden by the requesting party.

OIDC parameters in redirect URIs rejected by default

Redirect URIs (redirect_uri and post_logout_redirect_uri) containing OIDC response parameters such as state, code, or session_state are now rejected by default. These parameters conflict with the parameters that Keycloak attaches to the redirect URI after authentication or logout, potentially causing HTTP Parameter Pollution. This check also covers parameters in the URI fragment (used with response_mode=fragment).

If you used post_logout_redirect_uri pointing directly to the Keycloak OIDC login page (for example post_logout_redirect_uri=\https://mykchost/realms/myrealm/protocol/openid-connect/auth?response_type=code&client_id=myclient…​), replace it with a callback URL pointing to your application (for example post_logout_redirect_uri=\https://myapp/logout-callback) and ensure that opening that callback URL in your application redirects to Keycloak OIDC login.

To temporarily allow the previous behavior, enable the allow-oidc-params-in-redirect-uris server option in the OpenID Connect provider configuration. A per-client override is also available via the allow.oidc.params.in.redirect.uris client attribute, allowing gradual migration of individual clients. Both options are deprecated and will be removed in Keycloak 27.

Deprecated features

The following sections provide details on deprecated features.

Organization invitation link no longer exposed in API responses

The inviteLink field in OrganizationInvitationRepresentation is deprecated and is no longer returned in Admin REST API responses from the organization invitation endpoints.

Previously, the invitation link containing the signed registration token was stored and returned in the representation. The invitation link is still sent via email to the intended recipient.

The getInviteLink() and setInviteLink() methods on OrganizationInvitationRepresentation are deprecated and will be removed in a future major version. If your code reads inviteLink from invitation API responses, it will now receive null.

The "Copy invite link" action has also been removed from the admin console invitations management UI.

Migrating to 26.7.2

Breaking changes

Breaking changes are identified as those that might require changes for existing users to their configurations or applications. In minor or patch releases, Keycloak will only introduce breaking changes to fix bugs.

Legacy client-initiated account linking endpoint disabled by default

The deprecated legacy client-initiated account linking endpoint (/realms/{realm}/broker/{provider}/link) is now disabled by default. Migrate to Application Initiated Actions (AIA) with kc_action=idp_link. See Client initiated account linking for details.

To temporarily restore the previous behavior, see OpenID Connect provider configuration and enable allow-client-initiated-account-linking server option. This option is deprecated and will be removed in a future release.

Migrating to 26.7.1

Breaking changes

Breaking changes are identified as those that might require changes for existing users to their configurations or applications. In minor or patch releases, Keycloak will only introduce breaking changes to fix bugs.

Client scope assignment now enforces client scope permissions

The client scope assignment endpoints on a specific client now require the caller to have manage permission on client scopes, in addition to the existing manage permission on the target client. The listing endpoints for default and optional client scopes now return an empty list if the caller lacks view permission on client scopes.

If you have delegated administrators that assign client scopes using Fine-Grained Admin Permissions, ensure they also have the manage-clients realm admin role or a type-level manage permission on the Clients resource type.

Stricter checks in the Admin API for admin roles granted via protocol mappers

In this release, access to the Admin API is only granted to users with admin roles that are directly assigned to them, either through direct role assignments or group memberships. Admin roles granted via protocol mappers will no longer grant Admin API access.

This change was made to enhance security in realm administration delegation scenarios. Developers with limited realm administration permissions who have access to manage client protocol mappers or client scopes could previously map admin roles into tokens and gain unintended access to the Admin API. This is no longer possible.

If your existing deployment was relying on admin roles granted via protocol mappers to access the Admin API, you should update your configuration to directly assign admin roles to users or groups instead.

To verify if your deployment is affected, review any client protocol mappers that map admin roles to tokens. For each mapper found, ensure the affected users and service accounts have the required admin roles directly assigned either through direct role assignments or through group memberships. You can verify this in the Keycloak Admin Console by checking the user’s role assignments and group memberships in the target realm.

Notable changes

Notable changes may include internal behavior changes that prevent common misconfigurations, bugs that are fixed, or changes to simplify running Keycloak. It also lists significant changes to internal APIs.

Group hierarchy search returns stripped ancestors under Fine-Grained Admin Permissions

When Fine-Grained Admin Permissions is enabled and a delegated administrator has view permission on a group but not on its ancestors, the Admin REST API now returns the full group hierarchy with non-viewable ancestor groups represented as stripped representations. Stripped ancestor representations contain only id, name, path, parentId, and description — sensitive fields such as attributes, realmRoles, clientRoles, and access are omitted.

The subGroupCount field on stripped ancestors reflects the number of visible children included in the response, not the total number of children in the database. This prevents leaking information about groups the administrator cannot access.

In the admin console, non-viewable ancestor groups now appear greyed out and are not clickable in the group tree and breadcrumb navigation. Clicking a viewable child group navigates to its details as expected. Note that when a delegated administrator has view permission only on groups that are not at the top level, the Groups page initially appears empty. The administrator must use the search field to locate their permitted groups.

Migrating to 26.7.0

Breaking changes

Breaking changes are identified as those that might require changes for existing users to their configurations or applications. In minor or patch releases, Keycloak will only introduce breaking changes to fix bugs.

Test Framework representation builders

The test framework provides a number of builders to simplify creating representations. These can be used on their own, but are also used by RealmConfig, ClientConfig and UserConfig implementations.

Some builders had the suffix ConfigBuilder, while others had the shorter syntax Builder. They are all now renamed to use the shorter suffix Builder to keep things consistent.

Additionally, some methods were updated for consistency across the builders:

  • RoleBuilder method setSingleAttribute renamed to attribute

  • UserBuilder method roles renamed to realmRoles

  • RealmBuilder method client and addClient replaced with clients

  • RealmBuilder method user and addUser replaced with users

  • RealmBuilder method addRole replaced with realmRoles

  • RealmBuilder method addClientRole replaced with clientRoles

  • RealmBuilder method addAuthenticationFlow replaced with authenticationFlows

  • RealmBuilder method addClientScope replaced with clientScopes

  • RealmBuilder method identityProvider renamed to identityProviders

  • RealmBuilder method identityProviderMapper renamed to identityProviderMappers

  • RealmBuilder method requiredAction renamed to requiredActions

Identity Provider alias is now immutable

The alias of an Identity Provider can no longer be changed after creation via the Admin REST API. Attempts to update the alias will result in a 400 Bad Request error. Previously, although the admin console UI already treated the alias as read-only, the REST API still allowed alias changes.

Organization member query returns brief user representation by default

When querying organization members using the Admin REST API (GET /admin/realms/{realm}/organizations/{id}/members), a brief user representation is now returned by default. To retrieve the full user representation, pass ?briefRepresentation=false as a query parameter. The single-member endpoint (GET /admin/realms/{realm}/organizations/{id}/members/{member-id}) continues to return the full representation.

X509 client authentication requires CA Subject DN

Since this release, the X509 client authentication, which allows a client to authenticate by presenting a certificate in the mutual TLS (mTLS) handshake, requires configuring a new option called Certificate Authority subject DN to identify the trusted anchor CA for the client certificate. As the Keycloak’s trust-store (or mTLS store) is shared for all the realms, the new option allows identifying the correct CA of the certificate and avoids interference with other unrelated CAs.

The new option is compulsory in the administration console when configuring X509 credentials for the client. Currently, previous configurations without the CA subject DN still work to maintain backward compatibility. In the next major version, the validation will be present at the server side, making any update, creation, or import invalid if the new configuration option is not present. If you use the X509 authenticator for clients, ensure you add this new attribute to avoid issues in the future.

Additionally, the option Allow regex pattern comparison is deprecated. This option allows configuring a regexp instead of the exact DN of the client certificate. It is now deprecated and will be removed in a future version. The certificate for the client should be unambiguously identified.

For the client registration service, there is a new client policy executor named tls-client-auth-ca-subject-dn which can be used to assign a default CA subject DN. The executor can also enforce the configured CA DN to reject unwanted CAs.

In general, all this new configuration requires Keycloak to always revalidate the trusted anchors for the client certificate. This feature can be problematic in certain configurations (for example if a proxy is configured for TLS termination and the certificates are passed to Keycloak using headers). The trust-store or mTLS store in Keycloak should always be configured correctly to validate the client certificate chain. Please see Using a reverse proxy and Configuring trusted certificates for mTLS, for more information if you are using a TLS termination proxy and X509 client authentication.

The view-system admin role no longer exists

The view-system admin role, introduced in version 26.5.4, was immediately marked as deprecated. In this release, the role has been removed for security reasons.

Access to full server information is now restricted to users in the master realm holding the manage-realm role.

If you are still relying on the view-system role, you should consider accessing the full server information using a user (or service account) from the master realm and granted with manage-realm admin roles.

Before upgrading, you should check if any of your users, service accounts, or groups are still relying on the view-system role and review their access controls to ensure they have the necessary permissions to access the server information after the upgrade. The manage-realm admin role is a high-privileged role and should only be granted to highly-trusted accounts.

Notable changes

Notable changes may include internal behavior changes that prevent common misconfigurations, bugs that are fixed, or changes to simplify running Keycloak. It also lists significant changes to internal APIs.

Stricter validation of resource URI templates in Authorization Services

Resource URI templates used in Authorization Services are now validated more strictly when creating or updating resources via the Admin REST API. The documented patterns — named placeholders like /api/{id}/info, trailing wildcards like /api/*, suffix patterns like /*.html, and combinations like /api/{id}/files/* — continue to work unchanged.

The following malformed URI patterns, which previously were silently accepted but either never matched any URI or could cause runtime errors during authorization evaluation:

  • Empty placeholders, for example /api/{}/foo

  • Placeholder names containing a slash, for example /api/{client/id}/foo

  • Wildcards in the middle of a path, for example /api/*/info

  • Suffix patterns with an empty file extension, for example /*.

  • Stray closing braces outside a placeholder, for example /api/{id}/foo}/bar

Before upgrading, you can check for affected resources by querying the Admin REST API for each client that uses Authorization Services:

GET /admin/realms/{realm}/clients/{client-id}/authz/resource-server/resource

Inspect the uris field of each resource and verify that:

  • All {...} placeholders have matching braces, are not empty, and do not contain / in the parameter name.

  • Wildcards (*) appear only at the end of the URI as /* or as a suffix pattern like /*.html with a non-empty extension.

Resources with malformed URI templates should be corrected before upgrading. After the upgrade, existing resources with malformed templates will continue to be stored, but they may not match request URIs as intended. Any update to such a resource will require fixing the URI template at the same time, as the stricter validation applies to all create and update operations.

Organization invitation filters use exact matching

The email, firstName, and lastName query parameters on the organization invitations list endpoint (GET /admin/realms/{realm}/orgs/{orgId}/invitations) now use case-insensitive exact matching instead of substring matching.

If you rely on partial matching for these fields, use the search query parameter instead, which performs case-insensitive substring matching across email, firstName, and lastName fields.

Dynamic Scopes feature renamed to Parameterized Scopes

The experimental feature dynamic-scopes has been renamed to parameterized-scopes. If you enabled this feature using --features=dynamic-scopes, update your configuration to use --features=parameterized-scopes.

The corresponding Java model methods in ClientScopeModel (isDynamicScope(), setIsDynamicScope(), getDynamicScopeRegexp()) and constants (IS_DYNAMIC_SCOPE, DYNAMIC_SCOPE_REGEXP) have been deprecated in favor of their parameterized counterparts. The deprecated methods and constants will be removed in a future major release. The underlying database attribute names (is.dynamic.scope, dynamic.scope.regexp) are automatically migrated to is.parameterized.scope and parameterized.scope.regexp during upgrade.

Asynchronous commit on PostgreSQL databases

Transactions that update only ephemeral tables like persisted user sessions, client sessions, login failures, or events will use PostgreSQL’s async commit feature. Logouts will still force a synchronous commit.

You can opt out of this feature by setting --spi-connections-jpa--quarkus--async-commit=false

Parameterized scopes now require a parameter type

When the parameterized-scopes feature is enabled, creating or updating a parameterized scope via the Admin REST API now requires a parameterized.scope.type attribute. Available types are string, integer, boolean, username, and custom. The custom type additionally requires a parameterized.scope.regexp attribute with a valid regular expression. Requests using the old regexp-only format (without a type) are rejected.

During upgrade, existing parameterized scopes are automatically migrated to preserve their current behavior:

  • Scopes with the default <scope-name>:* regexp are set to the string type and the regexp is removed.

  • Scopes with a custom regexp are set to the custom type and the scope name prefix is stripped from the regexp.

  • Scopes with no regexp are set to the string type.

No manual action is required — the migration ensures existing scopes continue to work as before.

Verify email required before credentials setup during user self-registration

When user self-registration is enabled for the realm together with Verify Email, users will not see a password field by default on the registration screen. After registering their profile, the user is required to verify their email and then can set up a password (or optionally other credentials like OTP or Passkey) as a subsequent step. It is possible to disable this and keep the old behavior by enabling the switch Always set password on register form in the realm registration flow, on the Password validation authenticator. Note that this option is deprecated and exists mostly for backward compatibility. It may be removed in the future.

For the details, see the Server Administration Guide.

Socket timeouts and query timeouts for database connections

For all database connections, Keycloak now sets a socket read timeout matching the respective transaction timeout to prevent long hanging threads. For PostgreSQL databases, it additionally sets a query timeout.

Generated system truststore format follows the active crypto provider

The generated system truststore created from conf/truststores, --truststore-paths, and automatically discovered Kubernetes or OpenShift CA certificates now uses the generated truststore format preferred by the active crypto provider.

For most deployments, no configuration change is needed. Non-FIPS and FIPS non-strict mode continue to generate a PKCS12 truststore when PKCS12 is supported.

In strict FIPS mode, the generated system truststore now uses BCFKS instead of PKCS12. If you have automation that reads the generated truststore file directly from the data directory, update it to expect keycloak-truststore.bcfks with truststore type BCFKS instead of keycloak-truststore.p12 with truststore type PKCS12.

This change only affects the generated system truststore. Explicit truststore files configured with options such as --https-trust-store-file still use their configured type or the type detected from the file extension.

Configure TOTP and Update password required actions moved after Verify Email

In relation to the previous point, the required actions Configure OTP and Update password have been reordered to appear after Verify Email. This is done automatically for new realms or during a server update. If you prefer the old order, you can manually reorder them.

Shutdown timeout extended

Previous versions waited for one second for HTTP requests to finish during the shutdown of a node.

The current version extends the timeout to ten seconds by default. In setups that use distributed caches, it now also waits for caches to rebalance before shutting down, preventing a possible data loss. When there are no ongoing HTTP requests, and caches are already rebalanced, the shutdown will finish immediately as before. With that, we expect most customers to not observe a change in the behavior of Keycloak.

As before, for any rolling update or scaling up or down, this must be done one node at a time for all clustered setups of Keycloak.

Use the shutdown-timeout option to adjust the timeout to your environment. Restore the original behavior by setting it to 1s.

Extensions must not restart a Keycloak transaction

This change might affect custom Keycloak extensions.

The implicit transaction handling in Keycloak previously assumed that a transaction is started once per session, which is usually request-scoped. If it was started a second time, this might have led to an inconsistent state.

Starting with this version, this is now enforced and a session’s transaction can only be started once. This prevents difficult-to-track errors and lost commits. It also ensures that at the start of the transaction, all nested transactions are started if they have not been started earlier.

A transaction is started automatically before the REST endpoint code is invoked, even if the session was created earlier in a filter.

As before, transactions can be nested, which will pause the outer transaction once the nested transaction starts, and will resume the outer transaction once the nested transaction ends.

Organization group representations now include role mappings

Organization group representations returned by the Admin REST API now include realmRoles and clientRoles fields. Previously, these fields were null for organization groups. They are now empty lists/maps by default, and populated when roles are assigned to the group. If your code checks for null to detect organization groups or conditionally processes role mappings, update it to handle the new behavior.

Realm search now matches by display name

When searching for realms in the admin console, the search now also matches against the realm’s display name in addition to the realm name.

As part of this change, the displayName attribute has been promoted from a realm attribute to a dedicated column on the realm entity.

Before migrating, check the current value of the displayName attribute for your realms. If the current value of displayName attribute exceeds 255 characters, it will be truncated during migration.

LDAP binary attribute decoding is now configurable

The User Attribute LDAP mapper now includes a Decode Binary Attribute As configuration option that controls how binary LDAP attributes are decoded. The available values are:

  • auto (default for new mappers) — decodes as UUID when the LDAP attribute matches the configured UUID LDAP attribute (e.g. objectGUID in Active Directory), base64 otherwise.

  • base64 — always returns a base64-encoded string.

  • uuid — always decodes the value as a UUID/GUID string. If the binary value is not exactly 16 bytes (the size of a UUID), the decoder falls back to base64 encoding.

This change reverts a regression introduced in 26.6.1 where the objectGUID binary attribute format was silently changed from base64 to UUID string for user attribute mappers. Existing binary attribute mappers are automatically migrated to use base64 decoding to preserve their current behavior. If you were relying on the UUID string format introduced in 26.6.x, explicitly set the decoder to uuid after upgrading.

The Group LDAP mapper also includes a new Decode UUID Attribute to UUID Format toggle. When enabled, the UUID LDAP attribute (e.g. objectGUID in Active Directory) listed in "Mapped Group Attributes" is decoded to UUID string format instead of base64. New group mappers default to enabled (UUID format). Existing group mappers are automatically migrated to disabled (base64) to preserve their current behavior.

DPoP not supported for implicit and hybrid flows

DPoP (Demonstrating Proof of Possession) is no longer supported for implicit and hybrid flows. If a client has the Require DPoP bound tokens option enabled, authorization requests using implicit or hybrid response types will be rejected with an error.

This change improves security by preventing the use of DPoP with flows that issue access tokens in the front channel, where the sender-constrained token model cannot be properly enforced.

Token Exchange with Sender-constrained tokens allowed for scope changes

Standard Token Exchange now accepts sender-constrained tokens when a client is requesting an exchange of their own token. This change allows token audience changes and up/down scoping and is applicable to both DPoP-bound tokens and X.509 certificate-bound tokens (mTLS).

If you attempt to exchange a sender-constrained token using a different client, the request will be rejected with an invalid_request error. For more details, see the Token Exchange guide.

HAProxy certificate lookup provider

The HaProxySslClientCertificateLookup implementation has deprecated support for configuring --spi-x509cert-lookup—​haproxy—​ssl-cert-chain-prefix as HAProxy does not provide a built-in mechanism to forward individual intermediate certificates as dedicated HTTP headers. Consequently, this solution does not work when more than one intermediate certificate is used. Existing deployments of HaProxySslClientCertificateLookup will continue to work with ssl-cert-chain-prefix as before, but a deprecation warning will now be logged.

Users of ssl-cert-chain-prefix should instead make use of --spi-x509cert-lookup—​haproxy—​ssl-cert-chain. See the Reverse Proxy guide for more details.

Async Custom REST Endpoints should not be used

Async custom REST endpoints using AsyncResponse or returning an async type such as CompletableFuture or CompletionState should not be used. If you are using them, be aware that the KeycloakSession used to initiate the request will be immediately closed - it will not remain open for the entire lifespan of the request - and that you are fully responsible for transaction management in the async work.

KeycloakContext refinements when no request is active

The KeycloakContext provided by KeycloakSession for async work will typically not have an active request. The JavaDocs for methods now describe when a ContextNotActiveException is thrown, and some of the logic is now tolerant to returning values even if no request is active. Please review places where you are catching ContextNotActiveException in your extension logic against the new JavaDocs.

Service accounts excluded from user queries by ID

When querying users by ID through the Admin REST API, service accounts are no longer included in the results. Service accounts are internal to their associated clients and should not be exposed through general user queries. This aligns with the existing behavior for user search operations. If you need to access a service account, use the dedicated client service account endpoints.

FreeMarker configuration upgraded to version 2.3.32

The FreeMarker template engine now uses VERSION_2_3_32 defaults instead of the legacy 2.3.0 defaults. This brings improved security defaults and correctness fixes from newer FreeMarker versions. Custom themes that rely on legacy FreeMarker behavior — such as deprecated directives, undocumented template syntax, or accessing Java internals via ?api — may be affected. Test your custom themes after upgrading.

Client secret generation now generates longer secrets

Newly generated client secrets always use 86 characters to provide enough entropy for HS512 regardless of the configured token endpoint signing algorithm. Previously, the secret length varied based on the signing algorithm, which could result in secrets with insufficient cryptographic strength for some use cases. This increases the minimum length of generated secrets. Existing client secrets are not affected — only secrets generated after upgrading use the new length. If your application imposes a maximum length on stored client secrets, you may need to increase it. See #49793 for more details.

Token post-processors now respect ProviderFactory order

Custom OIDCAccessTokenResponseMapperProvider implementations are now invoked in the order defined by their ProviderFactory#order() method when processing token responses. Previously, the invocation order was undefined due to a bug, which could cause incorrect results when multiple post-processors depend on each other. If your deployment uses multiple custom token post-processors, verify that their order() values produce the expected sequence.

Custom provider changesets included in manual migration exports

When using --spi-connections-jpa—​quarkus—​migration-strategy=manual, the generated SQL file now includes changesets from custom internal JpaEntityProvider implementations. Previously, only Keycloak’s built-in changesets were exported. This change ensures that manual migration scripts contain all required database changes for custom providers.

RefreshTokenProvider SPI introduced

The refresh token processing logic has been extracted from TokenManager into a dedicated internal RefreshTokenProvider SPI. This refactoring makes refresh token handling pluggable, allowing custom extensions to override the default behavior through the standard SPI mechanism. Custom extensions that previously overrode or intercepted refresh token handling through TokenManager should be reviewed for compatibility. The default implementation (DefaultRefreshTokenProvider) preserves the previous behavior.

Default AES key size increased to 256 bits

The default key size for newly generated AES key providers (aes-generated) has been increased from 128 bits to 256 bits for improved security. CNSA 2.0 requires AES-256 for national security systems. AES-128 is not approved for classified data in the post-quantum era. Existing realms are not affected - only newly created key providers use the new default.

To migrate an existing realm, add a new aes-generated key provider with key size 32 and a higher priority. The old provider can be removed once all sessions using the old key have expired.

Login theme changes may affect custom themes

Several visual changes were made to the login theme in this release:

  • Action buttons across login theme pages now use a consistent horizontal layout.

  • The passkey authentication page and the signing-in page in the account console now display vendor-specific icons for registered passkeys and security keys, making it easier to identify the correct device.

If you use a custom theme that overrides these pages, verify that your customizations work as expected after upgrading.

Additional datasources may be excluded from health checks

When using multiple datasources, individual datasources can now be excluded from the health check endpoint. This allows deployments with optional or non-critical datasources to avoid false negatives in health monitoring. Read more about it in the Configure multiple datasources guide.

Session cookies now use SHA-384

The KC_AUTH_SESSION_HASH and KEYCLOAK_SESSION cookies are now hashed with SHA-384 instead of SHA-256. Although SHA-256 is still safe, CNSA 2.0 specifies SHA-384 as the minimum required hashing algorithm for national security systems.

No configuration change is required, and active sessions are not affected. After upgrading, an old KEYCLOAK_SESSION cookie no longer matches the new format and is silently replaced on the user’s next request. Users with a valid session stay logged in; only users whose session has already expired have to log in again, as they normally would.

Deprecated features

The following sections provide details on deprecated features.

Twitter Identity Broker deprecated for removal

In this release, the Twitter Identity Broker is deprecated for removal. It relies on Twitter’s legacy OAuth 1.0a endpoints.

As X (formerly Twitter) now supports OAuth 2.0, it is recommended to use the generic OAuth v2 identity provider configured against the X OAuth 2.0 endpoints instead. For the migration steps, see the Twitter section in the Server Administration Guide.

The broker remains enabled by default for backward compatibility. If you no longer use it, you can disable the twitter-broker feature using the features-disabled server option:

--features-disabled=twitter-broker

The twitter-broker feature is planned for removal in a future release.

Identity Brokering API V1 is deprecated

The Identity Brokering API V1 is now deprecated but remains enabled by default for backward compatibility. See the Release Notes for more details.

Switch Bearer-only on OpenID Connect clients is deprecated

The switch Bearer only on OIDC clients is now deprecated and will be removed in a future version.

The switch could be used to specify that a client acts only as a service, which cannot authenticate using any grant type or obtain tokens issued to itself. However, a bearer-only client can already be configured by creating an OIDC client without any grants supported.

The switch is already unavailable in the admin console client settings, but it was still possible to use it, for example, by directly updating a client through the admin REST API. In the future, this will no longer be possible.

Option Allow regex pattern comparison is deprecated for the X509 client authenticator

The option Allow regex pattern comparison is deprecated for the X509 client authenticator. This option allows configuring a Java regular expression instead of the exact DN for the client certificate subject DN. As noted in the breaking changes section, this option is now deprecated and will be removed in a future version. The certificate for the client should be unambiguously identified for security reasons.

Deprecation of SHA1 hashing functions in Keycloak 27

NIST is going to fully retire SHA1 hashing function in 2030. Keycloak will remove all uses of SHA1 in version 27.

Note

Consider SHA1 hashing retired in all uses within Keycloak. Users should configure other secure hashing functions as soon as possible (for example SHA2, SHA3).
WebAuthn policy option Require Discoverable Credential deprecated in favor of Discoverable Credential

The WebAuthn policy option Require Discoverable Credential (requireResidentKey) is deprecated and planned to be removed in the future. A new option Discoverable Credential (residentKey) is available in the WebAuthn Policy and WebAuthn Passwordless Policy, following the current WebAuthn specification. Unlike the deprecated Yes/No option, it supports the values required, preferred and discouraged, and is sent to the browser as authenticatorSelection.residentKey during the registration of WebAuthn credentials.

The deprecated option keeps working and is only used when Discoverable Credential is left as not specified. A warning is logged when the deprecated option is set to a concrete value. The corresponding realm representation attributes webAuthnPolicyRequireResidentKey and webAuthnPolicyPasswordlessRequireResidentKey are deprecated in favor of webAuthnPolicyResidentKey and webAuthnPolicyPasswordlessResidentKey.

Removed features

The following features have been removed from this release.

Experimental token-exchange-external-internal:v2 feature removed

The experimental and undocumented feature token-exchange-external-internal:v2 has been removed. This feature was superseded by the standard Token Exchange, which provides the same functionality. If you enabled this feature via --features=token-exchange-external-internal:v2, remove this configuration option and use the standard Token Exchange feature instead.

Batching option for persistent user sessions removed

The internal SPI options to enable writing updates to persistent sessions in batches have been removed. Batching has been disabled in previous releases by default, and its performance proved to be lower than with batching disabled, leading to its removal in this release.

The options spi-user-sessions—​infinispan—​use-batches and spi-user-sessions—​infinispan—​max-batch-size have been removed without replacement.

Migrating to 26.6.5

Breaking changes

Breaking changes are identified as those that might require changes for existing users to their configurations or applications. In minor or patch releases, Keycloak will only introduce breaking changes to fix bugs.

Stricter checks in the Admin API for admin roles granted via protocol mappers

In this release, access to the Admin API is only granted to users with admin roles that are directly assigned to them, either through direct role assignments or group memberships. Admin roles granted via protocol mappers will no longer grant Admin API access.

This change was made to enhance security in realm administration delegation scenarios. Developers with limited realm administration permissions who have access to manage client protocol mappers or client scopes could previously map admin roles into tokens and gain unintended access to the Admin API. This is no longer possible.

If your existing deployment was relying on admin roles granted via protocol mappers to access the Admin API, you should update your configuration to directly assign admin roles to users or groups instead.

To verify if your deployment is affected, review any client protocol mappers that map admin roles to tokens. For each mapper found, ensure the affected users and service accounts have the required admin roles directly assigned either through direct role assignments or through group memberships. You can verify this in the Keycloak Admin Console by checking the user’s role assignments and group memberships in the target realm.

Notable changes

Notable changes may include internal behavior changes that prevent common misconfigurations, bugs that are fixed, or changes to simplify running Keycloak. It also lists significant changes to internal APIs.

Migrating to 26.6.4

Breaking changes

Breaking changes are identified as those that might require changes for existing users to their configurations or applications. In minor or patch releases, Keycloak will only introduce breaking changes to fix bugs.

Files for the Java Keystore provider should be placed inside a fixed realm directory

The Java Keystore provider allows the realm administrator to load keys from an encrypted Java keystore file instead of the Keycloak’s database. Since this release, the path of the file is restricted to be inside a folder whose name should be the realm name and, in turn, this realm folder should be inside a fixed main directory for keystores (by default the data directory, ${kc.home.dir}/data). For example, for a realm called test, all keystore files should be located under ${kc.home.dir}/data/test. This way, Keycloak avoids leaking information from the File System and the keys are isolated between realms.

The main keystores directory, where the different realm folders are located, can be customized using a provider startup option:

kc.[sh|bat] start --spi-keys--java-keystore--keystores-path /path/to/keystores

When a keystore is created or updated, the keystore configuration option is checked to be a file under the commented realm folder. If the keystore is outside its boundaries, the operation fails. This new behavior means that existing keystores continue working but cannot be updated or modified until the file is copied to an allowed path. Imports from a previous configuration, with store files outside the realm folder, will fail in the same way.

If you are using a Java Keystore file to provide keys for a realm, please copy the keystore inside the appropriate realm folder and update the java-keystore provider configuration accordingly.

For more information, see Configuring realm keys chapter in the Server Administration Guide.

Notable changes

Notable changes may include internal behavior changes that prevent common misconfigurations, bugs that are fixed, or changes to simplify running Keycloak. It also lists significant changes to internal APIs.

Registration Access Tokens cannot be used when client is disabled

Registration Access Tokens (RAT) can no longer be used to retrieve, update, or delete a client when the client is disabled.

If you attempt to use a RAT to manage a disabled client, the request will be rejected with an HTTP 401 Unauthorized response. To manage a disabled client, you must use admin credentials instead of the client’s RAT.

Note that a RAT can still be used to disable a client, but once the client is disabled, the RAT can no longer be used to enable it again or perform other operations.

Migrating to 26.6.3

Breaking changes

Breaking changes are identified as those that might require changes for existing users to their configurations or applications. In minor or patch releases, Keycloak will only introduce breaking changes to fix bugs.

Valid redirect URIs for clients do not accept wildcards for hostname anymore

The wildcard comparison for valid redirect URIs does not affect the hostname anymore. Previously patterns like https://example.com* were accepted and lead to allow unintended domains like https://example.com.other.domain.com. Since this release, the hostname does not accept such type of wildcard and those patterns will be managed as if the wildcard is after the hostname https://example.com/*. The full wildcard for the hostname https://* is still accepted because its interpretation cannot be misunderstood.

Note that OAuth 2.0 recommends exact string matching in the Security Best Current Practice and draft for OAuth 2.1 enforces it. Keycloak recommends to not use any wildcard valid redirect URI for clients. See Unspecific redirect URIs in the Server Administration Guide for more information.

Client scope evaluation now enforces access to the user when generating tokens

In previous versions of Keycloak, client scope evaluation allow generating tokens without necessarily having the necessary admin roles or permissions to access the user.

In this release, client scope evaluation now requires at the very least the view-users admin role granted to the realm administrator or any permission that grants the view scope on the user.

User profile metadata no longer returned on user search

When searching for users using the Admin REST API, the user profile metadata is no longer included for each user in the search results.

This change was made to improve performance of user search operations, as fetching user profile metadata for each user can be resource-intensive. The user profile metadata can still be retrieved when fetching a specific user by their identifier.

Notable changes

Notable changes may include internal behavior changes that prevent common misconfigurations, bugs that are fixed, or changes to simplify running Keycloak. It also lists significant changes to internal APIs.

Maximum length of the token parameters in the OIDC token endpoint

When the OIDC token endpoint request (or OAuth2 token endpoint request) is sent, a new limit exists for the maximum length of "token" OIDC/OAuth2 parameter. The maximum length of each "normal" parameter is still 4,000 characters as in previous version. However maximum length of the "token" parameter is increased to 20,000 characters. Also when limit for the "token" parameter is reached, the parameter will not be ignored, but request will fail.

As "token" parameter is considered a parameter containing possibly long token (for example long JWT or SAML assertion) with unbounded data (For example possibly big amount of roles inside JWT). Applies for example for parameters like subject_token sent in case of the Token exchange grant.

If you want to increase or lower those numbers, start the server with the option req-token-params-default-max-size for the default maximum length of the OIDC/OAuth2 token parameter or you can use something such as req-params-max-size for one specific parameter. For more details, see the login-protocol provider configuration in the All provider configuration Guide.

CORS requests with an invalid Origin are now rejected before endpoint logic runs

Cross-origin requests sent to OIDC endpoints (such as the token, userinfo, token revocation, logout, and PAR endpoints) are now validated against the client’s configured Web Origins before the endpoint reaches its authentication and request-processing logic. Previously, requests carrying an Origin header that did not match any of the client’s allowed Web Origins could be processed up to the point where the response was about to be returned, which could result in inconsistent error responses depending on how far the request progressed.

After this change, such requests are rejected immediately with a CORS failure returning Forbidden (403) HTTP error status. There is no behavior change for same-origin requests (no Origin header) or for cross-origin requests whose Origin is already permitted by the client’s Web Origins configuration.

Extensions must not restart a Keycloak transaction

This change might affect custom Keycloak extensions.

The implicit transaction handling in Keycloak previously assumed that a transaction is started once per session, which is usually request-scoped. If it was started a second time, this might have led to an inconsistent state.

Starting with this version, this is now enforced and a session’s transaction can only be started once. This prevents difficult-to-track errors and lost commits. It also ensures that at the start of the transaction, all nested transactions are started if they have not been started earlier.

A transaction is started automatically before the REST endpoint code is invoked, even if the session was created earlier in a filter.

As before, transactions can be nested, which will pause the outer transaction once the nested transaction starts, and will resume the outer transaction once the nested transaction ends.

Validation of client_session_host parameter

The client_session_host parameter is now validated against the client’s registered nodes and management URL.

This parameter is sent by clients during token refresh and is used when the ${application.session.host} placeholder is present in a client’s backchannel logout URL configuration.

It was primarily used by legacy adapters that have been removed.

This improves security by ensuring that only trusted, pre-registered hosts can be used in backchannel logout URLs.

Migrating to 26.6.2

Breaking changes

Breaking changes are identified as those that might require changes for existing users to their configurations or applications. In minor or patch releases, Keycloak will only introduce breaking changes to fix bugs.

Token introspection now validates audience claim

The OAuth2 token introspection endpoint now validates that the authenticated client is present in the token’s audience (aud) claim before allowing introspection.

Previously, any authenticated client could introspect any valid token. Now, the introspection endpoint returns {"active": false} if the authenticated client is not in the token’s audience.

Migration

To temporarily restore the previous behavior, enable the backwards compatibility option either server-wide or per-client.

See OpenID Connect provider configuration to enable the server-wide configuration option allow-token-introspection-without-audience-check.

For the per-client option, navigate to Clients → select the client that performs the introspection → Advanced → OpenID Connect Compatibility Modes → enable Allow token introspection without audience check.

Note
 The per-client option is configured on the client that performs the introspection (the authenticated client), not the client that issued the token.
Warning
 Both server-wide and per-client options are deprecated and will be removed in a future release. They log warnings for each introspection request without audience validation.

Recommended migration path:

  1. After upgrade: Enable server-wide option temporarily to restore previous behavior

  2. Identify affected clients: Monitor server logs for warnings about introspection without audience validation

  3. Fix each client: Either add audience protocol mapper to include introspecting client in token’s aud, or enable per-client option

  4. Test and disable: Verify all introspection flows work, then disable server-wide option

Note
 For lightweight access tokens, the audience claim may only be present in the introspection response (not in the token itself). The audience check validates the transformed token returned by introspection, ensuring proper validation for both standard and lightweight tokens.
Note
 Refresh and offline tokens are not subject to the audience check, because their aud claim is the realm rather than a set of clients, and audience protocol mappers do not apply to them. Such a token can be introspected only by the client it was issued for (azp), and the compatibility options above do not relax that rule. Keycloak detects refresh and offline tokens from the token itself, so this applies whether or not token_type_hint is provided.
UserInfo endpoint rejects lightweight access tokens

The UserInfo endpoint now rejects lightweight access tokens by default. Lightweight access tokens contain minimal claims and are designed for token introspection.

Previously, lightweight access tokens were accepted by the UserInfo endpoint. Now, the UserInfo endpoint returns a 401 Unauthorized error when called with a lightweight access token.

Migration

To temporarily restore the previous behavior, enable the backwards compatibility option either server-wide or per-client.

See OpenID Connect provider configuration to enable the server-wide configuration option allow-userinfo-with-lightweight-access-token.

For the per-client option, navigate to Clients → select the client that issues tokens → Advanced → OpenID Connect Compatibility Modes → enable Allow UserInfo with lightweight access token.

Note
 The per-client option is configured on the client that issues the token (the azp claim).
Warning
 Both server-wide and per-client options are deprecated and will be removed in a future release. They log warnings for each UserInfo request with a lightweight token.

Recommended alternatives to lightweight tokens for UserInfo:

  1. Use token introspection instead: The token introspection endpoint is designed to work with lightweight tokens and will return the full claims

  2. Exchange lightweight token for full access token: Use token exchange to convert a lightweight token to a full access token, then call UserInfo with the full token

Notable changes

Notable changes may include internal behavior changes that prevent common misconfigurations, bugs that are fixed, or changes to simplify running Keycloak. It also lists significant changes to internal APIs.

WebAuthn acceptable AAGUIDs option restricts authenticators strictly

The WebAuthn policy presents the option Acceptable AAGUIDs to restrict the authenticators that are allowed to register new credentials. The AAGUID (Authenticator Attestation Global Unique Identifier) is an identifier for the authenticator’s type (e.g., make and model). This option requires the Attestation conveyance preference to be configured too (normally Direct), in order to force the authenticator to include the attestation inside the registration data.

Since this release, when this option is set up, the attestation is required to be present and signed with a valid certificate for the Keycloak trust-store. The None attestation format is explicitly not permitted. Previously, there were some corner cases in which a self attestation was accepted. The change is expected to be harmless, but maybe there are combinations of authenticators and WebAuthn policies that can present issues.

See chapter Managing policy in the Server Administration Guide for more information.

Workflow schedule synchronization across cluster nodes

A new cluster event was introduced to synchronize workflow scheduled settings across cluster nodes. You might see errors when updating workflow definitions during a rolling update when upgrading from 26.6.x. Defer updating workflow definitions until the rolling update has completed.

Required actions are one-time actions by default

Since this release, all Required Actions are now one-time actions by default to ensure they cannot be reused once completed. This change impacts the email action tokens created by the execute-actions-email endpoint, which is executed via the Credential reset button in the Admin console. Any action token generated through this endpoint (with whatever reset actions) is now strictly one-time use, ensuring that once a user completes the required steps, the token is immediately invalidated.

Migrating to 26.6.1

Manual migration if you migrated to 26.6.0

The migration to 26.6.0 might have introduced the Organization sub-flow to your custom browser flow in case they were bound to the browser flow binding type.

If you upgrade from 26.5.x or earlier directly to 26.6.1, this does not apply to you.

If you have migrated to 26.6.0, review your custom browser flow in each realm and remove the Organization sub-flow if this is not desired.

Once you upgraded to 26.6.1, you should see in the built-in browser flow the Organization sub-flow.

Breaking changes

Breaking changes are identified as those that might require changes for existing users to their configurations or applications. In minor or patch releases, Keycloak will only introduce breaking changes to fix bugs.

Outgoing HTTP connections do not follow redirects

Since this release, the HTTP connections originated from the Keycloak server do not follow redirects by default. The HTTP result will be the redirect status (3xx) code, and the Location header will not be automatically fetched. With this change, the server avoids retrieving unwanted redirects that can bypass allowed URLs defined by client policies or other future configurations.

The new option allow-redirects (SPI connections-http-client, provider default) is available to revert to the previous behavior. This option is deprecated and will be removed in a future version.

bin/kc.[sh|bat] start --spi-connections-http-client--default--allow-redirects true

See Outgoing HTTP requests documentation for more information.

Client scope evaluation now enforces access to the user when generating tokens

In previous versions of Keycloak, client scope evaluation allow generating tokens without necessarily having the necessary admin roles or permissions to access the user.

In this release, client scope evaluation now requires at the very least the view-users admin role granted to the realm administrator or any permission that grants the view scope on the user.

Migrating to 26.6.0

Breaking changes

Breaking changes are identified as those that might require changes for existing users to their configurations or applications. In minor or patch releases, Keycloak will only introduce breaking changes to fix bugs.

JavaScript-based policies can now only be deployed if the scripts feature is enabled

In this version, JavaScript-based policies can only be deployed if the scripts feature is enabled.

Until now, JavaScript-based policies were always enabled regardless of the scripts feature flag. However, this behavior was causing confusion as users expected that disabling the scripts feature would also disable JavaScript-based policies. It was also not aligned with how other script-based features work in Keycloak.

If you have existing JavaScript-based policies, make sure to enable the scripts feature when starting Keycloak.

Stricter Validation for Client URIs (secure-client-uris)

The 'secure-client-uris' client policy executor has been updated to enforce stricter validation on Client URIs. This aligns with FAPI 2.0 security requirements by mandating TLS (HTTPS) for client endpoints.

HTTPS Enforcement: The following URIs must now use the https scheme. Existing clients configured with http in these fields will fail validation during updates: 'Post logout redirect URIs', 'Logo URL', 'Policy URL', and 'Terms of Service URL'.

Expanded Wildcard Support: The + wildcard character is now allowed in 'Valid post logout redirect URIs' and 'Web origins'. Configuring + defers validation to the client’s standard 'Valid redirect URIs'. Since redirect URIs are already checked for HTTPS, using the wildcard maintains FAPI 2.0 compliance while reducing configuration duplication.

Administrators must ensure that all configured URIs for the fields listed above use https. Clients attempting to update or register with http in these fields will fail validation when using the 'secure-client-uris' executor.

The Identity Provider issuer should be unique for the JWT authorization grant and client assertions

The JWT authorization grant and the client assertion authentication use the issuer claim passed in the assertion to locate the Identity Provider defined in Keycloak, and that provider validates the claims and signature in the assertion. Since this version, the issuer configuration option must uniquely identify one provider. The grant operation or the assertion client authentication fails if more providers are returned for a single issuer. In the same way, the Identity Providers, when configured to allow the JWT authorization grant or client assertions, are validated to have a unique issuer defined in their configuration. Any update or add operation will fail if the issuer collides with another provider.

If you have identity providers defined with the same issuer and tested the JWT authorization grants or client assertions features, please update the providers accordingly before the upgrade. You can update the issuer or disable the provider to avoid the duplicates.

Endpoints are opened while Keycloak is initializing

Keycloak now opens its HTTP(S) and Management ports while initialization is still in progress when the health endpoints are enabled. This resolves problems with long-running migrations on platforms like Kubernetes where a migration might otherwise be aborted, leaving the database in a potentially inconsistent state.

If you use a proxy or load balancer, configure an HTTP health check with the path /health/ready to ensure traffic is only routed to the server once it is fully ready.

If this behavior is not desired or an HTTP health check is not possible, start Keycloak with --server-async-bootstrap=false to revert to the previous behavior where ports are opened only after initialization completes.

Notable changes

Notable changes may include internal behavior changes that prevent common misconfigurations, bugs that are fixed, or changes to simplify running Keycloak. It also lists significant changes to internal APIs.

Usage of second-class options

In prior releases, the introduction of a first-class option with a default would cause the second-class option (typically spi or quarkus properties) to no longer be used at all.

For example, spi-truststore—​file—​hostname-verification-policy was marked as deprecated after the introduction of tls-hostname-verifier. However, since tls-hostname-verifier included a default, direct usage of spi-truststore—​file—​hostname-verification-policy was no longer considered.

To ensure that the further introduction of new first-class options does not immediately break existing configurations, the logic for determining the value of a second-class option will now consider the direct usage of the second-class value ahead of a default, or derived default, from the first-class option.

Please review the server startup to see if there are any warnings about the continued usage of second-class options, and update them accordingly.

New option for transaction timeout

A new option transaction-default-timeout has been introduced to configure the default transaction timeout in Keycloak.
